How it meets the code: sync has no onlyWhenUnlocked modifier (src/PoolManager.sol:279-288), so anyone can set the synced currency and reserves at any point of a transaction, and the value stays until a settle or another sync in that transaction. collectProtocolFees checks the caller and then reverts only if the currency is an ERC-20 equal to the synced currency (src/ProtocolFees.sol:48-52). It never calls _isUnlocked, which is declared at src/ProtocolFees.sol:60 and implemented at src/PoolManager.sol:391-394 with no caller. ASSUMPTIONS.md A12 describes the guard as depending on the unlocked state; the code does not. I walked the fund-moving paths for a gain and found none. Collection of the synced ERC-20 is blocked regardless of lock state, so the balance cannot fall between a sync and its settle through this path. Native settlement uses msg.value and no balance (src/PoolManager.sol:353-354). Collection is bounded by protocolFeesAccrued with checked subtraction (src/ProtocolFees.sol:54-55) and the adapter fixes the recipient. A sync left behind by an earlier operation in a bundle is overwritten by the next sync and cleared by the next ERC-20 settle (src/PoolManager.sol:361). What remains is that a stale sync makes a later native settle with value revert with NonzeroNativeValue if its caller did not sync first (src/PoolManager.sol:356), and makes collection of that currency revert for the rest of the transaction.

What is at risk: No loss or lasting freeze found. Within one transaction, a party that runs earlier can make a later native settle or a fee collection revert.

What it takes: For the revert cases the two parties must share a transaction, for example an ERC-4337 bundle, and the later party must settle native currency without calling sync first. This is my own reading of the code and has not been checked by a second reviewer.

What can be done: Integrators should call sync immediately before every settle, including native, as the comment at src/PoolManager.sol:348 says. The deployer can decide whether sync should require the unlocked state and whether collectProtocolFees should check it, and can correct the A12 text either way.

Second reviewer's corrections: The code facts hold on every network. Only the permissionless controller is specific to mainnet. The repository's own tests expect the present behaviour, so the disagreement is between the code and two texts: the comment at src/interfaces/IProtocolFees.sol:40 and assumption A12. The synced check as written rejects collection in more situations than a check tied to the lock would, so the difference removes no protection.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:279-288`, `src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-57`, `src/ProtocolFees.sol:60`, `src/PoolManager.sol:391-394`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-364`.

Sources:

- https://sourcify.dev/server/v2/contract/1/0x89a5d5bf00a27d55c02951e49078a5c5771051db?fields=sources,abi,compilation (V4FeeAdapter source (exact match): collect(CollectParams[]) and triggerFeeUpdate(PoolKey) have no access modifier and call POOL_MANAGER.collectProtocolFees(TOKEN_JAR, currency, amount) and POOL_MANAGER.setProtocolFee(poolKey, fee))
- https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com (eth_call at block 26470380: PoolManager.protocolFeeController() = 0x89a5d5bf00a27d55c02951e49078a5c5771051db; adapter TOKEN_JAR() = 0xf38521f130fccf29db1961597bc5d2b60f995f85; adapter owner() and feeSetter() = timelock)
- https://sourcify.dev/server/v2/contract/1/0xf38521f130fccf29db1961597bc5d2b60f995f85?fields=sources,compilation (TokenJar has an empty receive function and a release function restricted to the releaser, so native fee collection does not hand control to an arbitrary party)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ethereum/ERCs/master/ERCS/erc-4337.md (a bundle is one transaction and transient storage persists across the UserOperations in it)

## F-054. Four interface comments and two test helper remarks disagree with the source, and none of them changes who can move funds

Info severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: all 21 networks.

What differs on the network: None. These are differences between the source text and the repository's own comments, tests and test doubles. They are the same on every network.

How it meets the code: This unit compared every NatSpec sentence in src/interfaces with the function it describes and read every helper in src/test and test/utils. Six statements do not match the source, in addition to the four recorded in F-006. First, src/interfaces/IHooks.sol:102 says the fee returned by beforeSwap is used only if it is at most 1,000,000. The source reverts with LPFeeTooLarge when the override flag is set and the fee is above the maximum (src/libraries/Pool.sol:303-305, src/libraries/LPFeeLibrary.sol:75-78). The test agrees with the source and expects the revert (test/DynamicReturnFees.t.sol:151-162), although its own comment says the large fee is not used. Second, IHooks.sol:50 and IHooks.sol:81 describe the delta handed to afterAddLiquidity and afterRemoveLiquidity as the sum of principal, fees and hook delta. The source passes principal plus fees, and subtracts the hook delta only after the hook returns (src/PoolManager.sol:171-178, src/libraries/Hooks.sol:219-243). The test doubles rely on the source behaviour (src/test/FeeTakingHook.sol:54-91, test/CustomAccounting.t.sol:270-322). Third, src/interfaces/IPoolManager.sol:85-86 calls the Swap event amounts the deltas of the pool's balances. The emitted values are the swapper's deltas, negative for the amount paid in (src/PoolManager.sol:241-250), and the tests expect -100 and 98 for an exact input of 100 (test/PoolManager.t.sol:500-503). Fourth, IPoolManager.sol:174 says take reverts if the requested amount is not available. That holds for native currency and for tokens with code, and test/PoolManager.t.sol:917-922 states it for an empty manager. For a currency address without code the transfer call succeeds with empty return data and nothing moves (src/types/Currency.sol:60-74), which earlier cycles recorded under assumption A4. Fifth, test/utils/CurrencySettler.sol:20-25 settles native currency without a prior sync and says a sync is not required, while the comment at src/PoolManager.sol:348 tells integrators to call sync before a native settle. A native settle that follows a token sync by other code in the same transaction reverts with NonzeroNativeValue (src/PoolManager.sol:353-356, test/PoolManager.t.sol:976-989). Sixth, src/test/ProtocolFeesImplementation.sol:27-33 offers setIsUnlocked and an _isUnlocked override, no test calls setIsUnlocked, and collectProtocolFees never reads the lock (src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-57). This is the test double side of the first item of F-006. I traced each item for a fund effect. A hook that returns an oversized fee can only stop swaps in its own pool. The delta seen by a liquidity hook is the amount the hook can charge against, and the caller delta plus the hook delta still equals the pool delta in every replayed case. The event wording affects readers of logs only. The native settle revert needs code that runs earlier in the same transaction, and it returns the caller's value with the revert.

What is at risk: No loss or freeze found. A hook author who trusts IHooks.sol:102 may ship a hook that makes swaps in its own pool revert when it computes a fee above 100 percent. An integrator that copies the native branch of the test helper without sync(address(0)) can have a native settle reverted by a token sync left earlier in the same transaction.

What it takes: None for the comments. The native settle revert needs a call to sync with a token address earlier in the same transaction, for example from a hook of a pool the caller used or from a contract that bundles several actions.

What can be done: Correct the six texts. Hook authors should cap the fee before setting the override flag. Integrators should call sync(address(0)) before every native settle, as the comment at src/PoolManager.sol:348 says.

Second reviewer's corrections: No correction to the six items. Two precisions. The fourth item says the comment holds for tokens with code. It holds only for tokens whose transfer reverts or returns false on a shortfall, because Currency.sol:68 also accepts empty return data from a contract with code. The actor value is nominal here. The record describes no loss or freeze. The two operational effects need a hook author for the fee revert and a party that runs earlier in the same transaction for the native settle revert, which F-014 already records.

Code: `src/libraries/Pool.sol:303-305`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:209-245`, `src/PoolManager.sol:171-178`, `src/PoolManager.sol:241-250`, `src/PoolManager.sol:348-365`, `src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-60`, `src/types/Currency.sol:60-74`.

Sources:

- src/interfaces/IHooks.sol:102 (Comment says an override fee above the maximum is not used.)
- src/libraries/Pool.sol:303-305 (The source validates the override fee and reverts with LPFeeTooLarge when it is above 1,000,000.)
- test/DynamicReturnFees.t.sol:151-162 (The test expects LPFeeTooLarge for an override fee of 1,000,001.)
- src/interfaces/IHooks.sol:50 (Comment says the delta passed to afterAddLiquidity includes the hook delta. Line 81 says the same for removal.)
- src/libraries/Hooks.sol:219-243 (The hook receives the delta before the hook delta is subtracted.)
- src/interfaces/IPoolManager.sol:85-86 (Comment describes the Swap event amounts as pool balance deltas.)
- test/PoolManager.t.sol:500-503 (The test expects amount0 -100 and amount1 98 for an exact input of 100 of currency0.)
- test/utils/CurrencySettler.sol:20-25 (The helper settles native currency without calling sync first.)
- src/PoolManager.sol:348 (Comment says integrators should call sync before a native settle.)
- src/test/ProtocolFeesImplementation.sol:27-33 (The test double has a lock toggle that no test uses.)

## F-055. Two more places where the repository's tests and test doubles disagree with the library source, and neither changes who can move funds

Info severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: all 21 networks.

What differs on the network: None. The disagreements are between files of this repository and hold on every network.

How it meets the code: This extends F-006 with what a replay of test/libraries, test/Tick.t.sol, test/types and the test doubles in src/test found. First, test/libraries/Pool.t.sol:73-83 expects SafeCastOverflow from Pool.modifyLiquidity only when a token amount is above 2^128 - 1. The variable is named maxInt128InTypeU256 and holds Constants.MAX_UINT128. The source casts both amounts to int128: Pool.sol:212-235 takes the signed results of SqrtPriceMath.sol:261-288 and SafeCast.toInt128 (SafeCast.sol:40-43) reverts once the amount of an add is above 2^127. For amounts from 2^127 + 1 to 2^128 - 1 the source reverts and the test expects success. One case from the replay: spacing 32767, price 735853766953812733719635225319847024411115828962 (tick 873548), range -131068 to 98301, liquidity 2300850342447660200206975412810193765, which is below the per tick cap 6076470837873901133274546561281575204. The amount of currency1 is 313585663563401618519606321096647269736 and the source reverts with SafeCastOverflow. Out of 400,000 random parameter sets 346 fell in this window. Every other outcome, including the order of the eight revert conditions at Pool.t.sol:51-70, matched the source. Second, src/test/SqrtPriceMathEchidnaTest.sol:144-197 asserts that the signed helpers return a positive amount for positive liquidity and a non-positive amount for negative liquidity. The source returns minus the rounded up amount for positive liquidity and the rounded down amount for negative liquidity (SqrtPriceMath.sol:266-270 and 283-287). On 200,000 random inputs the assertion for positive liquidity fails 135,655 times and the one for negative liquidity 48,354 times as written, and every other invariant of that file holds. The forge tests use the sign of the source: a negative delta is owed by the caller (PoolManager.sol:183 and 368-378), and test/libraries/StateLibrary.t.sol:300-301 negates the delta of an add to get the amount paid. The echidna harness is not part of forge test, so nothing in the repository runs it. Third, a correction to the fourth item of F-006: test/libraries/Pool.t.sol:135-158 calls swap for a nonzero amount only when it expects a revert. It makes no call for a valid limit and no call when a zeroForOne limit equals MIN_SQRT_PRICE, so that file says nothing about the boundary that Pool.sol:328-330 rejects and contains no successful nonzero swap. The third item of F-006 was replayed: 8,795 of 120,000 random cases had a zero amount with a 100 percent swap fee, where Pool.t.sol:129-131 expects InvalidFeeForExactOut and Pool.sol:311-320 returns a zero delta without touching state.

What is at risk: None on funds. In the first case the source refuses an add that the test would let through, so it is stricter than the test. A removal whose amount is 2^127 or more reverts at the same cast and has to be split, which an earlier cleared item on the int128 casts already covers. In the second case the source sign is the one the accounting in PoolManager needs: with the sign of the echidna file an add would credit the caller.

What it takes: None. No account gains anything from these disagreements.

What can be done: Change the bound at test/libraries/Pool.t.sol:73 to the int128 maximum and bring the sign assertions in src/test/SqrtPriceMathEchidnaTest.sol in line with SqrtPriceMath, so that the tests and the harness can serve as a reference for the source.

This entry adds to F-006.

Second reviewer's corrections: No party gains or loses through these disagreements, so the actor value carries no weight here; unprivileged is the value the existing F-006 entry already has. The counts 346 of 400,000, 135,655 and 48,354 could not be reproduced because the finding does not state its sampling distribution; the direction of each result reproduces. The third part repeats a correction that the cycle 1 verification of F-006 already recorded. The statement that nothing in the repository runs the echidna harness needs one addition: .github/workflows/mythx.yml:60 lists SqrtPriceMathEchidnaTest under contracts/test/, a directory that does not exist in this repository, and echidna.config.yml is present. No forge test runs the harness. On the limit for an add: an amount of exactly 2^127 passes, because the amount is negated before the cast and -2^127 fits int128. A removal passes up to 2^127 - 1.

Code: `src/libraries/Pool.sol:206-237`, `src/libraries/SqrtPriceMath.sol:261-288`, `src/libraries/SafeCast.sol:40-43`, `src/libraries/Pool.sol:311-330`.

Sources:

- test/libraries/Pool.t.sol:73-83 (The test expects SafeCastOverflow only above Constants.MAX_UINT128.)
- src/libraries/Pool.sol:212-235 (Both amounts of a liquidity change are cast with toInt128.)
- src/libraries/SafeCast.sol:40-43 (toInt128(int256) reverts when the value does not fit in int128.)
- src/test/SqrtPriceMathEchidnaTest.sol:144-197 (The echidna harness asserts a positive amount for positive liquidity.)
- src/libraries/SqrtPriceMath.sol:261-288 (The signed helpers return a negative amount for positive liquidity, rounded up, and a positive amount for negative liquidity, rounded down.)
- test/libraries/Pool.t.sol:129-158 (The swap fuzz test calls swap for nonzero amounts only when it expects a revert.)
- test/libraries/StateLibrary.t.sol:300-301 (The forge tests treat the delta of an add as negative.)

## F-003. A Stylus program used as a hook or token stops answering calls on expiry or a Stylus version change, and activation is blocked

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: author of a hook or token the victims use. Networks: Arbitrum One.

What differs on the network: On Ethereum, code at an address keeps answering calls. On Arbitrum One a contract can be a Stylus WASM program. A call to it fails unless the program was activated at the current Stylus version and its last activation or keepalive is less than 365 days old. ArbOS 61, active since 2026-08-20, moved the Stylus version from 2 to 3. On chain, ArbWasm.activationGas() is 2^64-1, which makes activateProgram fail for everyone, so a program that has expired or needs the new version cannot be brought back by its users.

How it meets the code: Hook path: the PoolManager calls the hook with call() and reverts the whole action when the call fails. For a hook address with the before or after remove liquidity flag, modifyLiquidity with a negative delta calls the hook, so a hook that no longer answers blocks every liquidity removal in its pools. Hooks with swap or donate flags block those actions the same way. Token path: transfer and balanceOf on a Stylus token that no longer answers revert, so take, sync and settle revert for that currency in every pool. Step 1: a pool is created with a Stylus hook or a Stylus token while the program is active. Step 2: the program reaches 365 days without a keepalive, or an ArbOS upgrade changes the Stylus version. getActiveProgram returns ProgramExpired or ProgramNeedsUpgrade and the call fails. Step 3: anyone who tries activateProgram is charged activationGas first, which exceeds any gas limit, so the call fails. Keepalive works only while the program is still active and at least 31 days old. ArbOS 61 also removed WASM multi-value support, so a program that relies on it cannot be reactivated even when activation reopens. I did not enumerate which pools on Arbitrum One use a Stylus hook or token, and I did not establish the date on which activation gas was raised, so I cannot say how many programs missed the move to version 3.

What is at risk: LP principal and fees in pools whose hook gates liquidity removal are stuck for as long as activation stays closed. For a Stylus token, every balance of that token in the PoolManager is stuck. No funds move to an attacker. Pools without Stylus hooks or tokens are unaffected.

What it takes: A pool must use a Stylus program as its hook or as one of its currencies. Then either nobody pays for keepalive within the 365 day window, or governance ships a Stylus version change. No attacker action is required and none can speed it up. The Arbitrum Foundation or Security Council can reopen activation.

What can be done: Pool creators on Arbitrum One should prefer EVM hooks. LPs in a pool with a Stylus hook should check ArbWasm.programVersion and programTimeLeft for the hook and for each token, and anyone can pay codehashKeepalive once the program is 31 days old. A hook author can leave the remove liquidity flags unset so that removals never depend on the hook.

Second reviewer's corrections: The mechanism and the chain facts hold. The scope needs two changes. First, no pool on Arbitrum One has a Stylus program as its direct hook or currency today, so no funds are frozen through the direct path at present. Indirect use through an EVM proxy or an EVM hook that calls a Stylus program was not checked. Second, the date the finding could not establish is 2026-10-02, 43 days after the version change. The same mechanism reaches Robinhood Chain. Its ArbWasm precompile emitted 26 ProgramActivated events between 2026-07-25 and 2026-10-02, all at version 3, and activation gas there is also 2^64-1. Those programs expire from 2027-07-25 unless someone pays keepalive, and a Stylus version change would stop them at once. On Robinhood Chain the PoolManager emitted 1,169,661 Initialize events up to block 120.6M. 411,764 of them name a hook, across 32,935 distinct hook addresses and 745,518 distinct currency addresses. None of the 26 activated program addresses is a hook or a currency of any pool. I read the code of the 1,500 most used hook addresses, which cover 377,624 of the hooked pools, and none starts with 0xeff0. I did not read the code of the remaining hooks or of the currencies, so a contract that shares its code hash with an activated program is not excluded there. The severity stays medium because the freeze is limited to pools that depend on a Stylus program and needs either a missed keepalive or a governance upgrade.

Code: `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:131-137`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:195-206`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:209-245`, `src/PoolManager.sol:145-184`, `src/types/Currency.sol:55-87`, `src/types/Currency.sol:90-96`.

Sources:

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OffchainLabs/nitro/v3.11.3/arbos/programs/programs.go (CallProgram calls getActiveProgram, which returns an error when the program version differs from the Stylus version or the program is older than ExpiryDays. ProgramKeepalive requires an active program at least KeepaliveDays old.)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OffchainLabs/nitro/v3.11.3/arbos/arbosState/arbosstate.go (The ArbOS 59 upgrade step calls UpgradeToVersion(3) on the Stylus parameters.)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OffchainLabs/nitro/v3.11.3/precompiles/ArbWasm.go (ActivateProgram burns the configurable activation gas before activation. CodehashKeepalive does not.)
- https://arb1.arbitrum.io/rpc eth_call ArbWasm.activationGas(), stylusVersion(), expiryDays(), keepaliveDays() at 0x71; queried 2026-11-22 (activationGas is 0xffffffffffffffff, the Stylus version is 3, expiry is 365 days and keepalive needs 31 days.)
- https://docs.arbitrum.io/notices/stylus-activation-pause-notice (New activations and reactivation of expired programs are paused on Arbitrum One. Active programs run until they expire and can be renewed with keepalive.)
- https://docs.arbitrum.io/run-a-node/arbos-releases/arbos61 (ArbOS 61 activated on Arbitrum One on 2026-08-20 and programs that rely on WASM multi-value can no longer be activated or reactivated.)
- https://docs.arbitrum.io/stylus/gentle-introduction (Stylus contracts must be reactivated once per 365 days or after any Stylus upgrade, and otherwise become uncallable.)
- https://forum.arbitrum.foundation/t/constitutional-aip-stylus-activation-contract/31605 (The proposal to reopen activation through an Arbitrum Foundation process was put on hold on 2026-11-18, and a later ArbOS proposal plans the ability to deactivate a Stylus contract.)

## F-004. A pool's hook can block or reprice every liquidity removal in that pool, and core has no path that skips the hook

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: both. Who can trigger it: author of a hook or token the victims use. Networks: all 21 networks.

What differs on the network: None. The code path is the same on every network. Networks where the code behind an address can change after pool creation (A7) add ways for a hook that was harmless at creation to start doing this.

How it meets the code: modifyLiquidity calls the hook library's beforeModifyLiquidity before it touches the pool (src/PoolManager.sol:156). For liquidityDelta <= 0 the library calls beforeRemoveLiquidity when address bit 9 is set (src/libraries/Hooks.sol:203-204). callHook reverts the whole call when the hook reverts (Hooks.sol:134-137) and when the return data is shorter than 32 bytes or carries the wrong selector (Hooks.sol:152-154). A hook address with no code returns empty data, so it also reverts. After the pool update, afterModifyLiquidity calls afterRemoveLiquidity when bit 8 is set, and when bit 0 is also set it reads a BalanceDelta from the return data, subtracts it from the caller's delta without any bound and credits it to the hook (Hooks.sol:232-243, src/PoolManager.sol:177-183). The hook call is skipped only when msg.sender is the hook itself (Hooks.sol:171-175, 217). No other function removes liquidity. The swap path has the same shape: beforeSwap and afterSwap deltas move value between swapper and hook (Hooks.sol:248-315), and the hook of a dynamic fee pool can set the LP fee to 100 percent (src/PoolManager.sol:339-346, src/libraries/LPFeeLibrary.sol:25, 37-45).

What is at risk: Principal and uncollected fees of every position in the affected pool. A reverting hook freezes them. A hook with the return delta bit can take part or all of what a removal would pay out, up to what the LP's router accepts. Funds in other pools are not reachable this way, because the caller delta plus the hook delta always equals the pool delta (Hooks.sol:230, 242, 312) and each party must net to zero before unlock returns (src/PoolManager.sol:112).

What it takes: The pool's hook address has bit 9 (0x200) or bit 8 (0x100) set, and for taking funds also bit 0. The hook code reverts, returns a bad selector, returns a hostile delta, or is replaced or removed after LPs deposit. LPs must have chosen that pool. This is how the hook design works; I list it because it is a path to a freeze or loss that the text of core does not limit.

What can be done: LPs and position managers can read the low 14 bits of the hook address before depositing and treat bits 9, 8 and 0 as custody risk unless the hook code is immutable and reviewed. Routers should enforce minimum amounts on removal so a hostile return delta reverts. The deployer cannot change this without adding a removal path that skips hooks.

Second reviewer's corrections: The difference field presents changing code as a property of some networks. EIP-7702 delegation and upgradeable proxy hooks make it possible on every network that has activated EIP-7702, Ethereum included, so the exposure does not depend on the network. The freeze case needs nothing from the LP. The taking case needs the LP's router to accept the reduced amount, as the finding says. The add liquidity path has the same shape through bits 11, 10 and 1, which can block or surcharge deposits and cannot hold existing funds. Severity stays medium because the loss is limited to pools whose LPs chose that hook address, and the tests show this is the intended trust model.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:145-184`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:131-155`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:171-175`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:195-245`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:248-315`, `src/PoolManager.sol:339-346`.

Sources:

- src/libraries/Hooks.sol:203-204 (beforeRemoveLiquidity is called for liquidityDelta <= 0 when the BEFORE_REMOVE_LIQUIDITY flag is set)
- src/libraries/Hooks.sol:134-154 (a failed hook call or a short or mismatched return reverts the caller's operation)
- src/libraries/Hooks.sol:232-243 (the afterRemoveLiquidity return delta is subtracted from the caller's delta with no bound)
- src/PoolManager.sol:177-183 (the hook delta is credited to the hook address and the reduced delta to the caller)

## F-005. Uncollected fees above the int128 maximum make every later update of a liquidity position revert

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: author of a hook or token the victims use. Networks: all 21 networks.

What differs on the network: None. The condition is the same on every network. It needs a token whose supply in base units is larger than 2^127 - 1, about 1.7e38. The comment at src/libraries/Pool.sol:403 says the code assumes token supply stays within uint128.

How it meets the code: Every modifyLiquidity call, including a zero delta poke and a removal, runs Position.update and then casts both fee amounts with feesOwed0.toInt128() and feesOwed1.toInt128() (src/libraries/Pool.sol:184-192). Position.update computes the fee as (feeGrowthInside - feeGrowthInsideLast) * liquidity / 2^128 for the whole position (src/libraries/Position.sol:92-97). SafeCast.toInt128(uint256) reverts for values of 2^127 or more (src/libraries/SafeCast.sol:56-59). Because the whole call reverts, the position's fee checkpoint is never updated. The amount owed therefore never goes down, and every later call for that position reverts the same way, whatever liquidityDelta is used. Fees reach a position through swaps (Pool.sol:401-407) and through donate (Pool.sol:466-480). Each single donate or swap is capped at int128, so the threshold is crossed over two or more calls. I confirmed the arithmetic with a Python transcription of Pool, Position and SafeCast: after two donations of 2^126 + 10 units to a range with one position, updates with delta 0, -1, -1e18 and +1 all revert.

What is at risk: The principal in both currencies and the uncollected fees of that one position stay in the PoolManager with no way out. Other positions in the pool and other pools are not affected.

What it takes: One position must be owed 2^127 base units or more of one currency in fees that it has not yet collected. Fees owed are real tokens paid by swappers or donors, so the token's supply in base units must exceed 1.7e38 and a payer must give up that amount. For a token with a supply below that bound the state cannot be reached. A party that controls such a token's supply can already drain the other side of the pool by selling, so this adds a way to freeze but no extra profit.

What can be done: LPs can avoid pools with a token whose base unit supply exceeds 2^127, and can collect fees with a zero delta call before the amount owed gets near the bound. Pool creators can check totalSupply and decimals. A code change that caps or splits the fee credit would remove the condition.

Second reviewer's corrections: Severity raised from low to medium. The scale reserves low for hazards with no direct loss path, and this is a direct and permanent freeze of a position, limited to pools with a token whose base unit supply exceeds 2^127 - 1. The actor is any holder of 2^127 base units who gives them up as a donation or as swap fees, or ordinary swap volume against a passive position that never collects. The bound is 2^127, half of the uint128 supply that the comment at src/libraries/Pool.sol:403 names as the assumption, so a token inside the stated assumption with a supply between 2^127 and 2^128 can still reach it. The finding's point that an issuer with unlimited supply can already empty the pool by selling holds, and it does not cover a third party token with a large fixed supply, where the attacker gains nothing and the LP still loses access to both currencies of the position.

Code: `src/libraries/Pool.sol:184-192`, `src/libraries/Position.sol:76-102`, `src/libraries/SafeCast.sol:53-59`, `src/libraries/Pool.sol:466-480`, `src/libraries/Pool.sol:401-407`.

Sources:

- src/libraries/Pool.sol:192 (both fee amounts are cast to int128 on every position update)
- src/libraries/Position.sol:92-97 (fees owed are computed for the full position liquidity and cannot be collected in parts)
- src/libraries/SafeCast.sol:56-59 (toInt128(uint256) reverts at 2^127)
- src/libraries/Pool.sol:403 (the code comment states the assumption that token supply does not exceed type(uint128).max)

## F-013. EIP-7702 lets the code behind a hook address be replaced or removed after a pool is created

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: both. Who can trigger it: author of a hook or token the victims use. Networks: Ethereum.

What differs on the network: Since Pectra (EIP-7702) an EOA can point its code at any contract through a delegation indicator, repoint it in a later transaction, or clear it. Since Dencun (EIP-6780) an ordinary contract cannot delete its code after the creation transaction. On mainnet today a delegated EOA is therefore the one kind of account whose code can change or vanish at a fixed address without a proxy pattern in its bytecode.

How it meets the code: initialize accepts a hook address on its low 14 bits alone and never looks at code (src/PoolManager.sol:126, src/libraries/Hooks.sol:109-127). An EOA whose address ends in chosen flag bits is cheap to find because only 14 bits must match. While the EOA delegates to honest hook code the pool behaves normally. If the account holder clears the delegation, a call to the hook succeeds with empty return data, and callHook reverts with InvalidHookResponse because the result is shorter than 32 bytes (src/libraries/Hooks.sol:131-155). With the before or after remove liquidity flag set, every liquidity removal by anyone other than the hook address then reverts (src/PoolManager.sol:156, 178; src/libraries/Hooks.sol:195-206, 232-243), so LPs cannot withdraw until the holder delegates again. If the holder repoints the delegation to hostile code and the address carries a returns-delta flag, the new code can assign itself part of each withdrawal or swap, because the hook delta is credited to the hook and subtracted from the caller (src/libraries/Hooks.sol:233-243, 298-313; src/PoolManager.sol:181-183, 224-226). The hook address is skipped when it is itself the caller (src/libraries/Hooks.sol:171-175, 217, 253, 293), and a delegated EOA can originate transactions. The holder can therefore delegate to code that answers the unlock callback for its own calls and returns invalid data to hook calls made for everyone else.

What is at risk: Funds of LPs and swappers in pools that use such a hook. Other pools are not affected because pool math never reads another pool's state. The same outcome is already possible with an upgradeable proxy hook; the change is that inspecting the code at the hook address at one point in time no longer shows it.

What it takes: The attacker creates the pool with a delegated EOA as hook and LPs deposit into it. I did not scan mainnet for existing pools whose hook is a delegated account.

What can be done: Interfaces, routers and LPs can read the code at the hook address and reject hooks whose code is the 23 byte delegation indicator starting with 0xef0100, or that have no code. The deployer could add a code check in initialize, but the same account could delegate afterwards, so the check belongs in periphery and off-chain listing.

Second reviewer's corrections: I raise the severity from low to medium. The finding describes a direct loss and freeze path for funds in pools that use such a hook, which is the medium definition, and it matches the rating of the general hook finding in the core-code-accounting unit, of which this is one variant. The statement that inspecting the code no longer shows the risk is too strong. A delegated account has 23 bytes of code starting with 0xef0100 and an undelegated one has none, and either result is a visible warning for a hook address. The freeze also covers swaps and donations when the matching before or after flags are set, since those calls go through the same callHook.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:126`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:109-127`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:131-155`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:171-175`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:195-245`, `src/PoolManager.sol:156-183`.

Sources:

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ethereum/EIPs/master/EIPS/eip-7702.md (an authority's code is set to 0xef0100 || address, can be changed by a later authorization and cleared by delegating to the zero address; delegated accounts may originate transactions; msg.sender == tx.origin no longer implies an account without code)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ethereum/EIPs/master/EIPS/eip-6780.md (SELFDESTRUCT no longer deletes code except in the transaction that created the contract)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ethereum/EIPs/master/EIPS/eip-7600.md (EIP-7702 is included in Pectra, with mainnet activation timestamp 1746612311)
- https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com (eth_config at block 26470380 reports fork id 0x07c9462e with activation time 1767747671, which EIP-7607 lists as BPO2, a fork later than Pectra)

## F-053. Activated Stylus programs exist on Robinhood Chain as well, and a copy of an activated program runs without a new activation

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: author of a hook or token the victims use. Networks: Arbitrum One, Robinhood Chain.

What differs on the network: Nitro chains run WASM contracts next to EVM contracts. A WASM contract answers calls only while its code hash is recorded as activated at the current Stylus version and the record is younger than the expiry period. Ethereum has no contract whose callability ends with time or with a protocol version. Cycle 1 recorded this for Arbitrum One and cleared Robinhood Chain on the ground that activation is closed there, without checking for earlier activations. Robinhood Chain has 26 activated code hashes, and the activation record is per code hash on both chains.

How it meets the code: The client looks a program up under the code hash of the called contract and fails the call when the record is missing, expired or at another Stylus version. Activation costs activationGas, which is 2^64-1 on both chains, so no new code hash can be activated and an expired one cannot be reactivated. A record can be extended with codehashKeepalive once it is 31 days old, by any account. An ordinary account can still deploy a new contract with the bytecode of one of the activated programs, at an address whose low 14 bits form a valid hook flag set, and name it as the hook or a currency of a pool. When the record of that code hash expires after 365 days without a keepalive, or when a later ArbOS release raises the Stylus version, every call to the contract fails. A failed hook call makes callHook revert, so each pool action that reaches the hook reverts, including liquidity removal when the address carries a remove flag. A failed transfer call makes take revert for that currency.

What is at risk: Funds in pools that use a Stylus program as hook or token. Liquidity providers cannot remove liquidity through a hook that no longer answers, and nobody can take a token that no longer answers. Pools without such a hook or token are not affected. On Robinhood Chain the 26 programs were activated between 2026-07-25 and 2026-10-02, so the first records expire in July 2027 unless someone extends them. I did not check whether any of the 26 programs or a copy of them is used by a pool.

What it takes: The pool creator must choose a Stylus program, or a copy of an activated one, as hook or currency, and the victims must use that pool. The freeze then needs the record to lapse or the Stylus version to change, which needs time or a chain upgrade.

What can be done: Liquidity providers should avoid pools whose hook or token is a Stylus program while activation is closed. Interfaces can read ArbWasm.programVersion and programTimeLeft for a hook or token address and warn when the answer is a live program. Anyone can call codehashKeepalive to extend a record that pools depend on.

This entry adds to F-003.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor and the severity stand. The actor is pool-creator because the victims must use a pool whose hook or currency the creator chose. The creator cannot bring about the lapse at will: it needs 365 days without a keepalive, which any account can pay while the record is live, or a Stylus version change by chain governance. Four additions. First, the two Arbitrum documents cover Arbitrum One and Nova. For Robinhood Chain the closed activation rests on the on-chain value of activationGas() and on the date of the last activation, and I found no statement by the chain operator. Second, a copy shares the record of its code hash. One keepalive extends the original and every copy, and a copy deployed late has only the time the original has left. Third, I checked the 26 program addresses on Robinhood Chain. None answers balanceOf(PoolManager) as a token would: 25 revert, and 0xd6cf26df204184fc9c3f2c47c36b04eb631ecf1f returns 64 bytes of hash output for any input and appears in no Initialize event as a currency between block 77,000,000 and the head. The low bits of 16 of the 26 addresses pass isValidHookAddress. The hook is not an indexed field of Initialize, so I did not establish whether a pool uses one of them, a copy, or an EVM contract that forwards to one. Fourth, the finding lists Hooks.sol:194-245 for liquidity changes. The same failure blocks swaps and donations for hooks with those flags, which the mechanism text implies and the references omit.

Code: `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:131-155`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:194-245`, `src/types/Currency.sol:55-87`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`.

Sources:

- https://rpc.mainnet.chain.robinhood.com eth_getLogs on 0x0000000000000000000000000000000000000071 and eth_call expiryDays(), keepaliveDays(), stylusVersion(), activationGas(), programTimeLeft(address), queried 2026-11-22 (26 ProgramActivated events with 26 distinct code hashes at Stylus version 3 on Robinhood Chain, expiry of 365 days, keepalive age of 31 days, activation gas of 2^64-1)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OffchainLabs/nitro/v3.12.4/arbos/programs/programs.go (CallProgram takes the code hash of the called contract and fails unless getActiveProgram finds a live record for it (lines 206 to 219 and 559 to 580))
- https://docs.arbitrum.io/notices/stylus-activation-pause-notice (new and expired programs cannot be activated while the pause lasts, active programs keep running until they expire and can be renewed with keepalive)
- https://forum.arbitrum.foundation/t/security-council-emergency-action-2-10-2026/31530 (the pause was made by setting the activation gas to 2^64-1 on 2026-10-02)

## F-018. On MegaETH a caller can make every block field and oracle read revert inside hook and token sub-calls, and one such read caps the rest of the transaction at 20 million compute gas

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: author of a hook or token the victims use. Networks: MegaETH.

What differs on the network: MegaETH treats block fields, the beneficiary account and Oracle storage as volatile data. Two rules follow that Ethereum does not have. First, any contract can call disableVolatileDataAccess() on the MegaAccessControl system contract at 0x6342000000000000000000000000000000000004. From then on every volatile read in that frame and in all frames below it reverts with VolatileDataAccessDisabled, uses no gas, and cannot be switched back on by a descendant. Second, when a volatile read does succeed, the whole transaction, including parent frames, may spend only 20,000,000 more compute gas, and the cap survives a revert of the frame that read.

How it meets the code: 1. The PoolManager source uses no block field (a search of src/ outside src/test found none), so its own code never triggers either rule. 2. A swapper's contract calls disableVolatileDataAccess() and then unlock (src/PoolManager.sol:104-114). The PoolManager, the hook it calls at src/libraries/Hooks.sol:131-155 and every contract the hook or a token calls are descendants of that frame. 3. A hook that executes TIMESTAMP or NUMBER in its own frame reverts, the call at src/libraries/Hooks.sol:134 fails and the PoolManager reverts at src/libraries/Hooks.sol:137. The swapper only stops its own transaction. 4. A hook that wraps the read in an external call and tolerates failure behaves differently. Examples are a price feed read with a staleness check, a read of the MegaETH Oracle or high-precision timestamp contract, or a call into a rate limiter, each inside try/catch with a default branch. The swapper can now force the default branch at will and at no gas cost. If that branch skips a circuit breaker, applies a base fee where a surge fee was due, or accepts a stale price, the swapper trades against liquidity providers on terms the hook was written to prevent. The same applies to tokens that catch failures of time-based accrual inside transfer. 5. Under the second rule a hook or token that reads a block field early leaves the PoolManager 20,000,000 compute gas for the rest of the transaction. The swap loop at src/libraries/Pool.sol:344-437 does a cold bitmap read per word and a tick update per initialized tick, so a single swap behind such a hook can cross several hundred initialized ticks before the budget ends. That figure is an estimate from Ethereum compute gas costs and was not measured. The swap can stop at a price limit (src/libraries/Pool.sol:344) and continue in another transaction, and liquidity removal touches two ticks (src/libraries/Pool.sol:146-238), so no funds are stuck.

What is at risk: Funds of liquidity providers in pools whose hook, or whose token, has a failure-tolerant path around a volatile read. No such hook was identified in this cycle, so no loss path is confirmed. Hookless pools and hooks that read block fields directly are unaffected apart from the 20,000,000 compute gas bound on work after the read.

What it takes: A pool must use a hook or token that catches a failed sub-call which reads a block field, the beneficiary or Oracle storage, and the fallback branch must be more favourable to the caller. Any account can then trigger it. The compute gas bound needs no precondition beyond a volatile read in the transaction.

What can be done: Hook authors on MegaETH should call isVolatileDataAccessDisabled() on MegaAccessControl at entry and revert when it returns true, or should never continue after a failed read. Reviews of hooks ported from other chains should list every try/catch and unchecked low-level call. Routers should set price limits so that large swaps behind time-reading hooks finish inside the detention budget.

Second reviewer's corrections: 1. Forcing the catch branch of a try around a sub-call is possible on any chain by starving the call of gas. MegaETH adds a way that costs nothing and always works, since the blocked frame returns all its gas. The finding should say this so the difference is not overstated. 2. The estimate of several hundred tick crossings inside 20,000,000 compute gas was not measured by the finding or by me. 3. A plain account cannot call disableVolatileDataAccess before unlock by itself. The caller has to be a contract or an account with delegated code. 4. Low stands. No loss path is shown, and the hazard sits in hooks and tokens outside this repository.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:104-114`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:131-155`, `src/libraries/Pool.sol:344-437`, `src/libraries/Pool.sol:146-238`.

Sources:

- https://docs.megaeth.com/spec/system-contracts/mega-access-control.md (disableVolatileDataAccess covers the caller's frame and all descendants; blocked reads revert without consuming gas; descendants get DisabledByParent)
- https://docs.megaeth.com/spec/megaevm/gas-detention.md (List of volatile reads, the 20,000,000 relative cap, transaction scope, persistence across reverted frames)
- https://docs.megaeth.com/spec/system-contracts/overview.md (MegaAccessControl address and Rex4 activation)
- rpc https://mainnet.megaeth.com/rpc, queries at blocks 32548426 to 32549054 on 2026-11-22 (397 bytes of code at 0x6342000000000000000000000000000000000004)

## F-040. Avalanche C-Chain folds two storage slot indexes into one, which lets a hook author hide a write path that Ethereum does not have

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: author of a hook or token the victims use. Networks: Avalanche.

What differs on the network: On the Avalanche C-Chain the node clears the lowest bit of the first byte of every storage slot index before SLOAD and SSTORE touch the account trie. Slot S and slot S with bit 248 flipped are therefore one slot. The set bit is reserved for balances of imported X-Chain assets. Ethereum, Polygon and BNB Smart Chain treat the two indexes as different slots.

How it meets the code: 1. The rule is in the client: partitionStateKeysHook.TransformStateKey clears bit 0 of byte 0, and libevm applies it in GetState, GetCommittedState and SetState. Transient storage is not rewritten. 2. The live PoolManager shows it. eth_getStorageAt for slot 0x01 followed by 31 zero bytes returns the owner address stored in slot 0, and extsload(bytes32) for that index returns the same word (src/Extsload.sol:10-15). 3. The PoolManager itself is not harmed. Its fixed slots are 0 to 6 (owner, protocolFeesAccrued, protocolFeeController, isOperator, balanceOf, allowance, _pools; src/libraries/StateLibrary.sol:11), all with a zero first byte. Every other slot is a keccak256 output or a keccak256 output plus an offset below 7 (src/libraries/Pool.sol:83-91, src/libraries/TickBitmap.sol:66-73, src/libraries/StateLibrary.sol:324-326). Two such slots collide only if two hashes agree in 255 bits, which costs about 2^127 hash evaluations to find even for a party that controls both inputs. Raw writes in src use slots derived this way (src/libraries/Pool.sol:544-557, src/libraries/TickBitmap.sol:73), and extsload only reads. 4. A hook or token is different, because its author picks constants. A contract can keep a guarded value, for example an admin or a fee recipient, at a hand-picked slot C and expose an innocent looking setter that writes slot C with bit 248 flipped. On Ethereum, and in any audit or fork test that runs standard EVM rules, the two slots are unrelated. On the C-Chain the setter overwrites the guarded value. 5. The PoolManager calls hooks with all gas and accepts their deltas (src/libraries/Hooks.sol:131-155), so a hook that gains control this way can do whatever its privileged path allows with the liquidity of its own pools.

What is at risk: No funds of pools without a hook are reachable. Liquidity in pools whose hook or token was reviewed on Ethereum semantics can be exposed to a storage alias that exists only on Avalanche. The size of the loss is whatever the aliased slot controls in that hook.

What it takes: The attacker must be the author of a hook or token that victims choose to use, and must place two constants that differ only in bit 248. No such contract was searched for on chain in this cycle. Honest contracts that use EIP-1967 or ERC-7201 slots are affected only if two of their constants differ in exactly that bit, which was not checked for deployed periphery.

What can be done: Reviewers of hooks and tokens for Avalanche should compare every constant storage slot after clearing bit 248 and should run fork tests against a C-Chain node. Interfaces can warn when a hook uses assembly sstore with constant slots. Core needs no change.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor pool-creator and the severity low are right. Three details. First, the mitigation that asks for fork tests against a C-Chain node is incomplete. A local EVM that loads state over RPC and executes SLOAD and SSTORE itself follows standard rules and would treat the two indexes as two slots. Only execution on an avalanchego node shows the alias. This is my reasoning from the source read and I did not run such a test. Second, the alias adds no power that a hook author lacks. A hook author can already write any privileged path into the hook. The alias only lets that path pass a review done on Ethereum rules. Third, eth_getStorageAt applies the same rewrite, so off-chain tools that read a hook's slot with bit 248 set see the value of the cleared index and cannot tell the two apart from the outside.

Code: `src/Extsload.sol:10-15`, `src/libraries/StateLibrary.sol:11`, `src/libraries/StateLibrary.sol:324-326`, `src/libraries/Pool.sol:83-91`, `src/libraries/Pool.sol:544-557`, `src/libraries/TickBitmap.sol:66-73`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:131-155`.

Sources:

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ava-labs/avalanchego/v1.15.3/vms/saevm/cchain/libevm/extstate/statedb.go (TransformStateKey calls storageKey, which clears bit 0 of byte 0. assetBalanceKey sets the same bit for imported asset balances.)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ava-labs/libevm/d398bc4dbe41/core/state/statedb.go (GetState, GetCommittedState and SetState pass the slot index through transformStateKey at lines 349, 359 and 422.)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ava-labs/libevm/d398bc4dbe41/core/vm/eips.go (opTload and opTstore use the slot index unchanged.)
- rpc https://api.avax.network/ext/bc/C/rpc, eth_getStorageAt and eth_call extsload(bytes32) on 0x06380c0e0912312b5150364b9dc4542ba0dbbc85, block 101078679 on 2026-11-22 (Slot 0x0100...00 returns 0x47eb0cf11a1626462da3c830bcde64c3f582b5a6, the value of slot 0.)
- rpc https://polygon.drpc.org and https://bsc-dataseed1.bnbchain.org, eth_getStorageAt on the PoolManager for slot 0x0100...00 on 2026-11-22 (Both return zero while slot 0 holds the owner, so the alias is specific to Avalanche within this unit.)

## F-051. A delegated session credential with a TIP-20 spending limit on Tempo can move the account's ERC-6909 claims and pool positions without limit

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: author of a hook or token the victims use. Networks: Tempo.

What differs on the network: Tempo accounts can authorise additional signers in the AccountKeychain precompile, with an expiry, optional call scopes and a spending limit per TIP-20 token. A transaction signed by such a signer runs with the account as msg.sender. The limit is enforced inside the TIP-20 precompile and only when the address that moves or approves tokens is tx.origin. Tempo's documentation states that assets other than TIP-20 tokens do not count and that a signer without scopes can call any contract. Ethereum has no protocol-level delegated signer.

How it meets the code: 1. A user holds ERC-6909 claims in the PoolManager, or liquidity positions through a periphery contract, and authorises a session signer for an application or agent with a limit such as 100 OUSD per day and no call scopes. 2. The holder of that signer sends a transaction as the user that calls transfer(receiver, id, amount) or setOperator(operator, true) on the PoolManager (src/ERC6909.sol:25-33 and 58-64). The PoolManager sees the user as msg.sender. No TIP-20 leaves the user's account, so AccountKeychain.authorize_transfer is never reached for the user. 3. The receiver or operator opens an unlock, burns the claims (src/PoolManager.sol:332-336, src/ERC6909Claims.sol:13-22) and takes the tokens (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297). The TIP-20 transfer has the PoolManager as from address, and authorize_transfer returns early because that address is not tx.origin. 4. The spending limit is never charged for any of these steps.

What is at risk: All ERC-6909 claims of that user in the PoolManager, and positions the user holds through contracts the signer may call, can be moved by a signer whose TIP-20 limit suggested a small bound. On 22 November 2026 the PoolManager held about 426,000 USD of TIP-20 tokens for all users together. Other users are not affected.

What it takes: The victim must have authorised a session signer without call scopes, or with scopes that include the PoolManager or a position contract, and the attacker must control that signer. The actor value is the nearest category: the attacker holds no chain role and needs a credential the victim chose to grant, comparable to a hook the victim chose to use. I confirmed the rule in the precompile source and the documentation and did not send a transaction.

What can be done: Users who keep claims or positions should give session signers explicit call scopes that exclude the PoolManager and position contracts. Wallets on Tempo can warn that a spending limit does not cover claims and positions.

Second reviewer's corrections: No actor value in the schema fits exactly. pool-creator is the nearest, because the victim must have granted the credential to the attacker, and the item is out of scope for the unprivileged goal. The 426,000 USD figure is the whole PoolManager balance and is an upper bound. I did not establish whether any account on Tempo holds ERC-6909 claims. The behaviour is documented by Tempo and is a property of its delegated credentials, which the PoolManager code cannot change. The same documentation sentence means that an allowance given to Permit2 before the credential was issued is also outside the limit.

Code: `src/ERC6909.sol:25-33`, `src/ERC6909.sol:58-64`, `src/ERC6909Claims.sol:13-22`, `src/PoolManager.sol:332-336`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`.

Sources:

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/tempoxyz/tempo/697ea97/crates/precompiles/src/account_keychain/mod.rs (authorize_transfer and authorize_approve return without a check when the account is not tx.origin or the transaction uses the root signer)
- https://tempo.com/developers/docs/accounts/access-keys/spending-limits.md (a spending limit covers TIP-20 transfers, approvals, fees, precompile pulls and burnAt, and assets other than TIP-20 tokens do not count)
- https://tempo.com/developers/docs/accounts/access-keys/scopes.md (a signer without scopes can call anything, and a call outside the scopes reverts with CallNotAllowed)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/tempoxyz/tempo/697ea97/crates/evm/src/handler.rs (execute_batch runs every call of the transaction with the account as caller and origin)

## F-059. Two pooled tokens make their transfer rules depend on the PoolManager address: ELO on Elysium needs a budget set by its hook, and v4CRCL on X Layer pays out only to permitted recipients

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: author of a hook or token the victims use. Networks: Elysium, X Layer.

What differs on the network: No chain rule is involved. These are token designs that exist on these two networks among the tokens the PoolManager holds. ELO at 0xe107e5d99910f544f88ef961e3ca6d88f06f29dd on Elysium stores the PoolManager address and has functions poolManagerGateEnabled, poolManagerTransferBudget, authorizePoolManagerTransfer, setPoolManagerGate, setHook and setBlockedVenue. v4CRCL at 0xd4f64a67b2c1295bd6c25b3a306b2a049e392397 on X Layer has POOL_MANAGER, PERMISSIONED_TOKEN, allowListChecker, allowedHooks, allowedWrappers, wrapToPoolManager and swappingEnabled, which is the shape of a wrapper for a permissioned token.

How it meets the code: 1. take(X, to, amount) debits the caller and makes the PoolManager call X.transfer(to, amount) (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297, src/types/Currency.sol:55-75). 2. ELO: a transfer of zero from the PoolManager succeeds. A transfer of 1 base unit or of 10^18 reverts with UnauthorizedPoolManagerTransfer(bool,uint256,uint256). authorizePoolManagerTransfer succeeds when called from the address returned by hook() and reverts for any other caller, including the PoolManager. Both budgets read zero outside a transaction and the bytecode contains TLOAD and TSTORE, so the budget appears to live in transient storage. ELO can therefore leave the PoolManager only in a transaction in which its hook has authorized the amount. A pool that names ELO with another hook or with no hook can receive ELO and can never pay it out, and an ERC-6909 claim on ELO can be burned for a take only when the hook takes part (src/PoolManager.sol:322-336). The owner of ELO can switch the gate and replace the hook. 3. v4CRCL: every transfer from the PoolManager to the four recipients I tried reverts with TRANSFER_FAILED, for a zero amount as well, and transfers by other senders revert with a custom error. Payouts depend on a permission list kept outside core. 4. In both cases the failed transfer makes take revert with ERC20TransferFailed (src/types/Currency.sol:82-86), and the same holds for collectProtocolFees (src/ProtocolFees.sol:56). I found no way for an ordinary account to use these rules against another currency: the checks act on the token's own balance, a zero amount poke changes nothing, and each take is debited in full.

What is at risk: The PoolManager holds 60,839 ELO on Elysium (about 46,000 USD of pool reserves on the tracker) and 65.98 v4CRCL on X Layer (about 1,800 USD). Holders of these tokens through pools or claims depend on the token owner and on the hook or permission list for every payout. Other currencies and other pools are not affected.

What it takes: A user must hold liquidity or claims in one of these tokens. The token owner, the hook or the permission list must refuse or stop authorizing. I could not obtain verified source for either token, so the function names come from the public signature database and the behaviour from read only calls.

What can be done: Front ends can flag currencies whose transfer from the PoolManager fails in simulation for a plain recipient. Liquidity providers should treat such a token like a hook that can block removal, which the state already records for all chains.

Second reviewer's corrections: The sentence that a pool with another hook or with no hook can receive ELO and can never pay it out is wrong for the receiving half. A transfer of 1 base unit to the PoolManager from each of four ELO holders reverts with UnauthorizedPoolManagerTransfer(true, 1, 0), so ELO can neither enter nor leave the PoolManager without a budget from the hook. The statement that the owner can replace the hook did not hold in simulation: setHook from the owner address reverts with 0xbef9156a, which the signature database names HookAlreadySet(). setPoolManagerGate(false) from the owner succeeds in simulation and reverts for another caller, so the owner can open the gate. The v4CRCL balance is now 68.54 and the finding quotes 65.98. Not confirmed: verified source for either token, the logic of the ELO hook and who controls it, and a payout of v4CRCL to a permitted recipient, because I did not find an address on the permission list. The actor value pool-creator fits ELO. For v4CRCL the party that decides payouts is the keeper of the permission list of the underlying security token, which is closer to issuer. Neither is an ordinary account. Severity low stands: the payout dependence on the hook is the hazard the state already records for hooks, and the sums are about 46,000 USD and under 2,000 USD.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/types/Currency.sol:55-86`, `src/PoolManager.sol:322-336`.

Sources:

- https://rpc.elysium.kinetiq.xyz (rpc eth_call at block 9,697,391 and later on 2026-11-22: ELO transfer(recipient, 0) from the PoolManager returns true; transfer of 1 and of 10^18 revert with 0x206e5f37; poolManagerGateEnabled() is 1; poolManager() is the PoolManager; hook() is 0x34a6e76a10881d215abb5872f584bef6e3103dcd; authorizePoolManagerTransfer(true, 10^18) succeeds from the hook address and reverts with 0x67373552 from two other senders; balanceOf(PoolManager) is 60,838.98 ELO; a linear scan of the 7,456 byte code finds 5 TLOAD and 3 TSTORE)
- https://rpc.xlayer.tech (rpc eth_call at block 76,621,774 and later on 2026-11-22: v4CRCL transfer from the PoolManager to the Universal Router, the PositionManager and two plain addresses reverts with TRANSFER_FAILED for amounts 0 and 1000; balanceOf(PoolManager) is 65,979,861 with 6 decimals)
- https://api.4byte.sourcify.dev/signature-database/v1/lookup?function=0xe7d0c09e,0x206e5f37 (selector 0x206e5f37 is UnauthorizedPoolManagerTransfer(bool,uint256,uint256); the same database resolves the ELO and v4CRCL function selectors named in this finding)
- https://api.geckoterminal.com/api/v2/networks/elysium/dexes/uniswap-v4-elysium/pools?page=1&include=base_token,quote_token (ELO is among the twelve largest currencies of Uniswap v4 pools on Elysium by tracked reserve)

## F-069. Tokens whose balance falls with time leave the PoolManager short of what pools record: TURING on Ethereum has lost 73 percent of its pooled balance in 263 days

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: author of a hook or token the victims use. Networks: Ethereum, Robinhood Chain, Arbitrum One.

What differs on the network: These are token designs with a balance that decays by a time formula. Nobody has to send a transaction for the balance to fall, and no Transfer log is emitted.

How it meets the code: 1. Pool accounting records amounts and never reads balanceOf again. take pays out recorded amounts with a plain transfer (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297, src/types/Currency.sol:40-87). 2. Thirteen tokens named TURING on Ethereum lost 0.5 percent of their PoolManager balance in the one day window with no log. For 0x11fb7c5e6fe7e42fb9e28929b673d3eee20048d4 getDecayInfo(PoolManager) returns a stored amount of 999,999,999.99 tokens, a current balance of 267,590,219.70 and 263 days of decay. The contract has checkIn(uint256,bytes), lastCheckIn and hook() among its selectors, so holders who check in keep their balance and the PoolManager cannot. 3. Once withdrawals and swaps have paid out the 267.6 million that exist, every later take of this token reverts in the transfer, and the last positions cannot be closed in that currency. 4. On Robinhood Chain three tokens named PYRE fell 5.3 to 5.6 percent in the window and SMK2 fell 0.39 percent. On Arbitrum One DDL fell 3.7 percent. All without logs.

What is at risk: Each TURING token has a supply of 1,000,000,000 and the PoolManager holds about 26.8 percent of it after decay, 5.3 percent for two of them. The stored amount for the token I read equals almost the whole supply, so the liquidity is the deployer's own. The other balances are small: 9.34 PYRE, 2.12 SMK2, 3.34 DDL. I found no third party funds at risk today. The pattern matters for any future pool in a token with demurrage.

What it takes: A pool in a token whose balance decays. No attacker is needed. The TURING source is not verified; I read selectors from bytecode and state through its getters. I did not read the pool positions, so the split between the deployer and others is inferred from the stored amount.

What can be done: Do not pool tokens with a decaying balance unless the token exempts the PoolManager address. Interfaces can detect them with the reconcile script.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor value pool-creator is acceptable: the hazard comes from the token and hook that the pool's creator wrote, and only pools in that token are affected. Nobody takes anything, so the effect is a freeze of the last withdrawals in that currency. One detail to add: isExcluded(PoolManager) returns true on the token I read while the balance still decays, so that flag does not exempt the PoolManager from decay. The statement that the liquidity is the deployer's own is inferred from the stored amount, as the finding says, and I did not check it either.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/types/Currency.sol:40-87`.

Sources:

- src/PoolManager.sol:291-297 (take debits the caller and transfers before anything is paid in.)
- src/types/Currency.sol:40-87 (The payout is a transfer call that reverts with ERC20TransferFailed when the token refuses it.)
- https://mainnet.gateway.tenderly.co (eth_call on 0x11fb7c5e6fe7e42fb9e28929b673d3eee20048d4: getDecayInfo(PoolManager) returns 999999999999999999999997062, 267590219702701977999999213, 263, 732409780297298021999997849, 0. balanceOf(PoolManager) at blocks 26,467,827 and 26,475,001 for the thirteen tokens is in research/network-risk/cycles/004/theme-balance-reconcile.work/raw/mismatch_1.json.)
- https://robinhood.drpc.org (eth_call balanceOf(PoolManager) at blocks 120,299,181 and 121,117,362 for PYRE (three addresses) and SMK2, read twice; no Transfer log naming the PoolManager between them. Rows in research/network-risk/cycles/004/theme-balance-reconcile.work/raw/mismatch_4663.json.)

## F-074. More pooled tokens gate their own transfers on the PoolManager address: AGL on eight networks, PLAYR, MOON, INFX, A3K, LFNX, FIN and four more Securitize wrappers

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: author of a hook or token the victims use. Networks: Ethereum, Optimism, BNB Smart Chain, Polygon, Base, Arbitrum One, Avalanche, Celo, Robinhood Chain, Elysium, X Layer.

What differs on the network: No chain rule is involved. These tokens store the PoolManager address and allow a transfer to or from it only inside an operation of their own hook or for permitted parties. AGL (AgroLastro, 0x0000000000109de411eabc9f04906d79f06fda0e, the same address on Ethereum, OP Mainnet, BNB Smart Chain, Polygon, Base, Arbitrum One, Avalanche and Celo) calls its hook contract PoolAGL on every transfer that touches the PoolManager, and the hook keeps a signed quota in transient storage that only its afterSwap and its own fee collection open. PLAYR on Robinhood Chain reverts with V4NotAuthorized. MOON on Elysium reverts with selector 0x971d2ee1 and the PoolManager address for every nonzero amount. INFX on Avalanche accepts transfers into the PoolManager only up to an allowance that its tax hooks credit in the same transaction. A3K on OP Mainnet marks limited accounts, which receive only with an exception that its own contracts grant, and a transfer back to the PoolManager reverts with AccountIsLimited. LFNX on OP Mainnet and FIN on Polygon revert with the same selector on the return leg, and I did not read their source. v4MSTR, v4COIN, v4HOOD and v4VOLOROST2 on X Layer have the selector set of v4CRCL, which F-059 describes.

How it meets the code: 1. take(X, to, amount) and a payment before settle both end in X.transfer or X.transferFrom with the PoolManager as one party (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297 and 349-364, src/types/Currency.sol:60-75). 2. AGL: _update calls pool.consumirSaida(amount) when the sender is the PoolManager and pool.consumirEntrada(amount) when the recipient is. Both revert with ForaDaCota(amount, quota) unless the quota that afterSwap opened in this transaction covers the amount. From the PoolManager address a transfer of 1 AGL reverts with ForaDaCota(1e9, 0) on Ethereum, Base, Polygon and Celo, and cota() reads 0. The PoolManager holds between 99.9999 and 100 percent of the 1e21 AGL minted on each of the eight networks. 3. A take of AGL therefore works only in a transaction that also swaps in the official pool, and an ERC-6909 claim on AGL can be burned for a take only then (src/PoolManager.sol:331-336). The free round trip of this unit is not available for these tokens, because the return leg is refused. 4. The quota of AGL is one transient value for the whole transaction. It stays set when an unlock ends with claims minted in place of a take. I did not test what a later user of the same transaction meets, which belongs to the unit on shared transactions. 5. For the other tokens the failed transfer makes take revert with ERC20TransferFailed (src/types/Currency.sol:82-86) in the same way.

What is at risk: Holders of ERC-6909 claims and any pool that names one of these tokens without the hook the token expects cannot pay the token out. For AGL the liquidity belongs to the issuer and third party liquidity is refused by the hook, so the exposed parties are claim holders and integrators. No path for an ordinary account to block other users was found.

What it takes: A user holds a claim or a position in such a token outside the flow the token author intended. The token author or hook author sets the rules.

What can be done: Interfaces should not offer ERC-6909 claims or hookless pools for tokens that read the PoolManager address in their transfer logic. Pool creators should simulate a transfer from the PoolManager address before pairing a token.

This entry adds to F-059.

Second reviewer's corrections: Actor pool-creator and severity low stand. The statement that no ordinary account can block other users holds across transactions, because every gate is transient. Inside one transaction the AGL value is shared: a buyer who ends an unlock with claims leaves a negative value, and a later sale of AGL in the same transaction then fails consumirEntrada, which requires a value that is at least the amount. The research file records this as a lead for the shared transaction unit. The entries for MOON, LFNX, FIN and the four X Layer wrappers rest on the researcher's calls alone.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:322-336`, `src/types/Currency.sol:60-86`.

Sources:

- https://celo.blockscout.com/api/v2/smart-contracts/0x0000000000109de411eabc9f04906d79f06fda0e (Verified source of AGL: _update calls pool.consumirEntrada or pool.consumirSaida when the PoolManager is a party; the same address is used on eight networks.)
- https://celo.blockscout.com/api/v2/smart-contracts/0x0000000456b76bc801253ef2366664750f602840 (Verified source of PoolAGL: the quota lives in transient storage, afterSwap adds the AGL delta, consumirEntrada and consumirSaida revert with ForaDaCota, beforeAddLiquidity refuses third parties.)
- rpc eth_call on the endpoints of chain ids 1, 8453, 137 and 42220 at blocks 26,475,539, 61,094,554, 97,802,546 and 83,508,526 (transfer(recipient, 1e9) from the PoolManager address reverts with 0x7c467237 (ForaDaCota) and quota 0; cota() returns 0. eth_getCode of the AGL address has 15,125 bytes on chain ids 1, 10, 56, 137, 8453, 42161, 43114 and 42220 and balanceOf(PoolManager) is about 1e21 AGL on each.)
- https://sourcify.dev/server/v2/contract/4663/0x775fd5a987bf3ff5e205deabde6634ddcee3a3fc?fields=sources,proxyResolution,compilation (Verified source of PlayrToken: _enforceTradeGates reverts with V4NotAuthorized when the PoolManager is a party and no authorisation is set.)
- https://api.routescan.io/v2/network/mainnet/evm/43114/etherscan/api?module=contract&action=getsourcecode&address=0xda7ad1b3423713dc9b4275a00d59b82024b3eb0c (Verified source of InferenceToken (INFX): transfers into the PoolManager are limited to a transient allowance credited by its tax hooks, error NoHookAllowance.)
- https://explorer.optimism.io/api/v2/smart-contracts/0x6F3852B4622497EEE563cBA022977B8162bFD3fa (Verified source of IndexTokenV4 behind A3K: limited accounts receive only with an exception, error AccountIsLimited.)
- rpc eth_call with state override on https://rpc.elysium.kinetiq.xyz, https://rpc.mainnet.chain.robinhood.com, https://rpc.xlayer.tech, https://mainnet.optimism.io and https://polygon.drpc.org, 2026-11-23 (Observed reverts from the PoolManager address or back to it: MOON 0x971d2ee1 with the PoolManager address, PLAYR 0x19e7dcd0, v4MSTR, v4COIN, v4HOOD and v4VOLOROST2 TRANSFER_FAILED, A3K, LFNX and FIN 0xb8b82b49 on the return leg.)

## F-002. Robinhood Stock Tokens and USDG let the issuer block, pause, burn or upgrade the tokens held by the PoolManager

High severity, confirmed. Effect: both. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: Robinhood Chain.

What differs on the network: The assets this chain is built for are issuer-controlled in a more concentrated way than a typical Ethereum token. All Robinhood Stock Tokens are beacon proxies behind one contract, 0xe10b6f6b275de231345c20d14ab812db62151b00, which is both the upgrade beacon and the access registry with isBlocked, blockAccounts, pause and upgradeTo. The shared logic contract has adminBurn(address,uint256) and pause. USDG is an upgradeable Paxos token with isFrozen and decreaseSupplyFromAddress. The PoolManager already holds both.

How it meets the code: Step 1, block or pause: if the registry blocks the PoolManager address or a token is paused, the token's transfer call fails. Currency.transfer treats a failed call as an error and reverts with ERC20TransferFailed, so every take and every collectProtocolFees in that token reverts. Settling needs a transfer into the PoolManager, which a pause or a block on the recipient would stop as well. One registry entry covers every stock token at once, and A6 applies because one address holds all pools. An LP can still remove liquidity, take the other currency of the pair and call mint to hold the frozen side as an ERC-6909 claim, because mint moves no tokens. The claim cannot be turned into tokens until the block is lifted. Step 2, burn: adminBurn against the PoolManager lowers balanceOf(PoolManager) with no matching delta. Pool reserves, claims and accrued fees then exceed the balance. Takes keep succeeding until the balance runs out, and the last LPs and claim holders bear the shortfall. Step 3, upgrade: upgradeTo on the beacon changes the code of every stock token in one transaction, which removes the A4 expectation that token code is fixed. I read function selectors and view results from chain. I did not read verified source, so the exact transfer check against the registry and the role holders are unconfirmed. Stock token splits and dividends do not create a balance change, because they move uiMultiplier and leave balanceOf fixed.

What is at risk: At the time of the query the PoolManager held 3,134.87 ANT stock tokens and 16,984,261 USDG. A block or pause freezes the stock token side of every pool that uses the affected tokens. A burn creates a loss equal to the burned amount, carried by whoever withdraws last. The other currency in each pair stays withdrawable.

What it takes: The issuer (Robinhood Assets (Jersey) Limited for stock tokens, Paxos for USDG) or a holder of the relevant role must act. isBlocked(PoolManager) and isFrozen(PoolManager) are false today and both tokens are unpaused. No unprivileged party can trigger any of these functions.

What can be done: The deployer can ask both issuers to confirm in writing how the PoolManager address is treated and can monitor the registry and beacon for block, pause and upgrade events. Pool creators and LPs should size stock token and USDG positions with a freeze and a shortfall in mind. A later cycle should read the verified source of the logic and registry contracts.

Second reviewer's corrections: The transfer check is now confirmed from verified source and it also covers the recipient, so a take of a stock token to a blocked recipient reverts for that caller only. The role holders are single accounts with no code, which the finding did not establish. For USDG the direct burn path is narrower than stated: decreaseSupplyFromAddress works on another address only for a supply controller flagged to burn from any address, and it rejects frozen addresses. The seizure path for a frozen address is wipeFrozenAddress in the facet, whose source I did not read. USDG is a claimable rewards token. Its balanceOf excludes unclaimed rewards and a claim moves tokens from a claim source to a destination set per payout group, so the PoolManager balance does not grow with time. I did not check whether the PoolManager is registered in a payout group. Balances moved slightly since the finding was written: 3,134.38 ANT and 16.84M USDG.

Code: `src/types/Currency.sol:55-87`, `src/types/Currency.sol:90-96`, `src/PoolManager.sol:279-288`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:322-329`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-365`, `src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-57`.

Sources:

- https://rpc.mainnet.chain.robinhood.com eth_getStorageAt (beacon slot), eth_getCode and eth_call on 0xbbB6c29bCc3a1028A2B54310DF73F4F7A3bcB373, 0xe10b6f6b275de231345c20d14ab812db62151b00, 0xb35490d6f9163de4f80d88dc75c3516eb64c5ae2 and 0x5fc5360D0400a0Fd4f2af552ADD042D716F1d168; queried 2026-11-22 (Beacon and registry are one contract, the selectors listed are present in the bytecode, isBlocked and isFrozen return false for the PoolManager, paused() is false, and the PoolManager balances are as stated.)
- https://api.openchain.xyz/signature-database/v1/lookup?function=0x6abf7081,0xfaed47fd&filter=true (Selector names blockAccounts(address[]) and unblockAccounts(address[]). The same service resolved the other selector names quoted.)
- https://docs.robinhood.com/chain/building-with-stock-tokens/ (Stock tokens are 18 decimal ERC-20s. The ERC-8056 multiplier changes on corporate actions while balanceOf and totalSupply stay fixed.)
- https://docs.robinhood.com/chain/stock-tokens/ (Stock Tokens are debt securities issued by Robinhood Assets (Jersey) Limited, which also acts as tokenizer.)
- https://docs.robinhood.com/chain/contracts/ (Canonical WETH and USDG addresses on Robinhood Chain.)
- https://cdn.robinhood.com/assets/robinhood/legal/rhj_final_terms_for_tokenised_debt_securities_linked_to_anthropic_pbc.pdf (0xbbB6c29bCc3a1028A2B54310DF73F4F7A3bcB373 is the securities ledger contract of a Robinhood stock token. Read through a search result excerpt only.)

## F-011. One issuer blocklist entry on the PoolManager address freezes or wipes a stablecoin for every pool

High severity, confirmed. Effect: both. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: Ethereum.
