## F-065. A TIP-20 issuer has already frozen a pooled token on Tempo by setting the always reject transfer policy

High severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: Tempo.

What differs on the network: A TIP-20 token carries a transfer policy id that its admin can change at any time. Policy 0 is built in and rejects every sender and recipient.

How it meets the code: 1. GABAGOOL at 0x20c000000000000000000000030a4c7f7b8f3586 was used as a currency in two pools of the PoolManager, which still holds 600,000 base units (0.6 tokens). 2. transferPolicyId() on the token now returns 0, and isAuthorized(0, PoolManager) is false. 3. take calls transfer on the token (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297, src/types/Currency.sol:55-86). The precompile reverts with PolicyForbids and the take reverts with the wrapped ERC20TransferFailed error. I simulated a take of 1 unit and of the full balance. Both revert. 4. A holder of a position in those pools can still remove liquidity, take the other currency and turn the GABAGOOL delta into an ERC-6909 claim or clear it (src/PoolManager.sol:310-329). The GABAGOOL itself cannot leave. 5. Of the 73 currencies that Tempo pools name, this is the only one where a take of the PoolManager balance fails today.

What is at risk: This instance concerns 0.6 tokens of a token with a supply of 310.6. It shows that the issuer control described in F-031 is used in practice on this network. The same single call by the policy admin of OUSD or pathUSD would stop takes of 406,692 OUSD and 19,629 pathUSD.

What it takes: The admin of the token calls changeTransferPolicyId, or the admin of the policy changes its lists. No action by an ordinary account produces this state.

What can be done: List the transfer policy id and its admin for each pooled TIP-20 in the deployment notes and monitor changes. Liquidity providers should treat TIP-20 tokens with an active admin as revocable.

This entry adds to F-031.

Second reviewer's corrections: Both GABAGOOL pools carry the hook 0x55aeb9ebfa15c28ff9d9a675e8574a5bbe186aa8 (21,648 bytes of code). Its address has the beforeInitialize, beforeAddLiquidity, beforeRemoveLiquidity, beforeSwap, beforeDonate and beforeSwapReturnsDelta flags, so step 4 of the mechanism also depends on that hook accepting the removal (src/libraries/Hooks.sol:195-206). The finding does not mention the hook. TIP20Factory.create_token has no role check (crates/precompiles/src/tip20_factory/mod.rs lines 98 to 149), so the admin of GABAGOOL is whoever created it. For a token that an attacker issues and that victims then choose to pool, the schema class is pool-creator. The class issuer stays right for pathUSD, OUSD and the other stablecoins. The cited registry file holds the interface only. The checks that a transfer runs and the admin check for a policy change are in crates/precompiles/src/tip20/mod.rs. pathUSD and OUSD share policy 2, a blacklist with one admin, so one entry for the PoolManager stops takes of both tokens at once. The OUSD balance is 408,832 at my block, where the finding has 406,692.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/types/Currency.sol:55-86`, `src/PoolManager.sol:310-329`.

Sources:

- https://rpc.mainnet.tempo.xyz (eth_call transferPolicyId() returns 0 on the token, isAuthorized(0, PoolManager) returns false, balanceOf(PoolManager) returns 600000, and a simulated take through unlock reverts with 0x54cfe659 inside the WrappedError of the PoolManager)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/tempoxyz/tempo/697ea97/crates/contracts/src/precompiles/tip403_registry.rs (the registry interface with WHITELIST, BLACKLIST and COMPOUND policies and the authorisation checks a transfer runs)

## F-007. Scheduled Jenner fork adds protocol-native tokens whose issuer can seize, pause or block the PoolManager balance

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: both. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: BNB Smart Chain.

What differs on the network: BNB Smart Chain has scheduled the Jenner fork for 2026-12-08 02:30 UTC. It ships BEP-702, a token standard named CAS20 that runs as native code behind addresses starting with 0xCA52. Every CAS20 token has a role that can reassign a holder's balance (seizeWithMemo), a per-feature pause, and transfer policies that read shared allowlists and blocklists. BEP-703 lets governance list these tokens as payment assets with reserved block space. Ethereum has no token logic in the protocol.

How it meets the code: 1. The PoolManager keeps every pool's reserves, all ERC-6909 claims and accrued protocol fees for a currency in one balance, read by balanceOf at src/types/Currency.sol:94 and paid out by transfer at src/types/Currency.sol:60-75. 2. A CAS20 token keeps the transfer and balanceOf selectors, so pools can be initialized with it. initialize makes no call to the currency (src/PoolManager.sol:117-142). 3. The token admin points SEIZE_HOLDER_POLICY at a blocklist that contains the PoolManager address. An account with SEIZE_ROLE then calls seizeWithMemo(PoolManager, to, value). The balance moves without any take, and no delta is recorded. Pool accounting still shows the old reserves, so the last liquidity providers and claim holders to exit find transfer failing for lack of balance. 4. If TRANSFER_SENDER_POLICY stops authorizing the PoolManager, or the TRANSFER feature is paused, the token reverts every transfer from the PoolManager. Currency.transfer then reverts at src/types/Currency.sol:82-86, so take (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297) and collectProtocolFees (src/ProtocolFees.sol:56) fail for every pool that uses the token. 5. If TRANSFER_RECEIVER_POLICY stops authorizing the PoolManager, nobody can pay the token in, and settle credits nothing (src/PoolManager.sol:358-360). 6. The Asset variant multiplier does not change balanceOf, so it creates no rebase at the PoolManager. That part was checked and is safe.

What is at risk: All reserves, ERC-6909 claims and protocol fees held in the affected CAS20 token across every pool. Seizure is a loss for liquidity providers of those pools. A policy change or pause is a freeze that lasts until the issuer reverses it. Liquidity providers can still remove liquidity, take the counter-asset and hold the affected token as an ERC-6909 claim through mint (src/PoolManager.sol:322-329), because mint makes no token call.

What it takes: Jenner activates as scheduled, chain governance opens CAS20 creation through the ActivationRegistry, a CAS20 token is created, and pools holding it exist on the PoolManager. The issuer roles of that token must then act. An unprivileged attacker can do this only with a token it issued itself, which harms only those who pool against that token. None of these conditions hold today. The factory and registry addresses have no code at the time of the query. The BEP is still marked Draft and the client implementation was not read, so the final behaviour could differ from the text.

What can be done: Treat CAS20 tokens as issuer-controlled assets in interfaces and routing. Before listing a CAS20 pool, read the token's six policy ids, its pause mask and the holders of SEIZE_ROLE and the admin role. Prefer tokens whose role membership is frozen with no seizer. Liquidity providers should size positions on the assumption that the issuer can take or freeze the pooled balance. After the fork, re-test take and settle against a CAS20 token on a fork node.

Second reviewer's corrections: 1. The client on master names the holder scope SEIZE_EXEMPT_POLICY (core/vm/cas20_admin.go), while the BEP text names it SEIZE_HOLDER_POLICY. The semantics are the same. Tools that watch the PoolManager's seizability must use the getter the client exposes. 2. The finding attributes the hazard to issuer roles only. BEP-702 section 3.8 makes the PolicyRegistry a shared singleton, so the admin of a policy list can change the PoolManager's status for every token that references the list without holding a role on any of them. 3. The finding says the client implementation was not read. It is on bnb-chain/bsc master and agrees with the BEP on transfer, pause, policy checks and seizure. 4. Severity stays medium. The actor is a token issuer, which the scale places at high. No CAS20 token exists today, the PoolManager holds none, and the loss is limited to pools of the affected token. Raise it to high once a CAS20 token with a seizer or a restrictive sender policy has a balance at the PoolManager.

Code: `src/types/Currency.sol:60-75`, `src/types/Currency.sol:82-86`, `src/types/Currency.sol:94`, `src/PoolManager.sol:117-142`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:322-329`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-365`, `src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-57`.

Sources:

- https://github.com/bnb-chain/BEPs/blob/master/BEPs/BEP-702.md (CAS20 design: stateful precompiles, address space, seizeWithMemo semantics, policy scopes with ALWAYS_ALLOW defaults, pause features, multiplier leaves balanceOf unchanged)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/bnb-chain/BEPs/master/BEPs/BEP-702.md (Full text read for sections 3.6, 3.8, 3.9, 3.12, 3.15 and 6.7)
- https://github.com/bnb-chain/BEPs/blob/master/BEPs/BEP-718.md (Jenner contains BEP-702, BEP-703 and BEP-706, and token creation is opened by a governance-appointed switch)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/bnb-chain/bsc/master/CHANGELOG.md (Client v1.8.2 schedules mainnet Jenner for 2026-12-08 02:30:00 UTC)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/bnb-chain/bsc/master/params/config.go (Mainnet JennerTime is 1796697000)
- https://github.com/bnb-chain/BEPs/blob/master/BEPs/BEP-703.md (Payment lane lists stablecoins and BEP-702 native tokens by governance vote)
- rpc https://bsc-dataseed1.bnbchain.org, queries near block 135065254 on 2026-11-22 (eth_getCode returned empty code for 0xCA5F000000000000000000000000000000000000, 0x7020000000000000000000000000000000000002 and 0x0000000000000000000000000000000000002007)

## F-043. stHYPE and wstHYPE are two currency ids over one share balance, so a loss in the rebasing face is paid from the shares of pools in the other face

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: both. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: HyperEVM.

What differs on the network: The StakedHYPE liquid staking token on HyperEVM has two ERC-20 contracts that read and write the same storage. stHYPE at 0xfFaa4a3D97fE9107Cef8a3F48c069F577Ff76cC1 stores shares with 24 decimals and reports shares times totalSupply divided by totalShares. wstHYPE at 0x94e8396e0869c9F2200760aF0621aFd240E1CF38 keeps no balances: its balanceOf returns stHYPE.sharesOf divided by 1e6 and its transfer calls stHYPE.transferShares. Every holder has both balances at once, and a transfer through either contract changes both. The issuer's docs describe this and warn that multi entry point tokens have caused losses elsewhere. The stHYPE figure rises every second, because totalSupply is interpolated over an interval of 82,800 seconds, and a rebase to a lower supply applies in the same transaction. On Ethereum, stETH and wstETH are separate tokens with separate balances.

How it meets the code: The PoolManager treats the two addresses as unrelated currencies. Deltas, pool reserves and ERC-6909 claims are recorded per currency address in that currency's unit (src/libraries/CurrencyDelta.sol:10-41, src/PoolManager.sol:322-336). A take under either id calls transfer on that contract and moves shares out of the single balance the PoolManager holds (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297, src/types/Currency.sol:55-87). Let S be the shares the PoolManager holds, W the amount owed under the wstHYPE id in shares, T the amount owed under the stHYPE id in stHYPE units, and r the stHYPE per share rate. The PoolManager can pay everyone while S is at least W plus T divided by r. I first checked what an ordinary account can do. A credit under either id needs a rise of that contract's balanceOf between sync and settle (src/PoolManager.sol:279-288 and 349-365). Both balances rise only when shares arrive, since r depends on block.timestamp and is constant inside one transaction (stHYPE.sol lines 495 to 513) and Overseer.rebase reverts for a caller without REBASER_ROLE. One deposit therefore cannot be credited under both ids, and no account can move r inside its own sync window. Rounding between the two units is at most one base unit per settle. Three effects remain. First, T is a fixed number while r grows, so the staking yield on the shares that back stHYPE pools and claims stays in the PoolManager and no pool or claim can withdraw it. Second, when the rebaser reports a lower supply, r falls at once (stHYPE.sol lines 400 to 404). T does not change, so paying T now takes more shares than were paid in. If the PoolManager also holds shares for wstHYPE pools, those takes succeed by using them, and the wstHYPE pools end up short by T times the difference of 1/r after and 1/r before. An account that sees the slash coming (the Overseer exposes pendingSlashExists) can deposit under the stHYPE id before the rebase and withdraw after it, and gains that difference on its deposit. Third, pauseTransfer on stHYPE blocks transfer and transferShares, so one pause stops take under both ids.

What is at risk: No funds are exposed today: sharesOf(PoolManager) is zero. If pools or claims exist under both ids, a slash of x percent moves about x percent of the amount owed under the stHYPE id from the LPs of wstHYPE pools to the accounts that withdraw stHYPE first. LPs of stHYPE pools lose the staking yield on their stHYPE, about 2 percent a year at the current rate. A pause freezes every pool that uses either id.

What it takes: Liquidity or claims under both ids in the PoolManager. The loss needs a rebase to a lower supply, which only a holder of REBASER_ROLE can send and which follows a validator slash; Hyperliquid has no automatic slashing at present. The yield loss needs no actor. I did not read the Overseer implementation at 0x2b8698dd4b8addf7ddcabc46c6aedafe5a70b11c, which is not verified on Sourcify. That no public function other than rebase starts a rebase rests on the simulated revert of rebase for an unprivileged caller and on the last rebase transaction.

What can be done: Interfaces and routers on HyperEVM should offer wstHYPE pools only and refuse pools whose currencies include the stHYPE address. LPs should not provide stHYPE through the rebasing contract. The PoolManager cannot call setSelfDisableTransfer on stHYPE with any of its call shapes, so excluding the stHYPE face needs a change in core or a refusal at the interface level. Monitoring should watch stHYPE for Rebase events whose new supply is below the current supply and for TransferPaused.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor issuer is correct for the loss shift and for the freeze, since both need a call by a holder of REBASER_ROLE or PAUSER_ROLE, and the slash that would lead to a lower supply is not available to an ordinary account. An ordinary account that deposits under the stHYPE id before such a rebase gains only if that rebase happens. Medium fits because the PoolManager holds neither token today and the loss needs pools or claims under both ids. The statement about slashing is backed by the Hyperliquid staking page, which the finding did not cite.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:279-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:322-336`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-365`, `src/types/Currency.sol:55-87`, `src/libraries/CurrencyDelta.sol:10-41`.

Sources:

- https://docs.valantis.xyz/stakedhype/integrate/wsthype (Issuer statement that stHYPE and wstHYPE are two interfaces to the same data, that a transfer of one transfers the other, and the warning about multi entry point tokens)
- https://sourcify.dev/server/v2/contract/999/0xe71caf5c1fe56d8897c7b604295d23968049e057?fields=sources (Verified stHYPE implementation (exact match): shares, transfer, transferShares, syncSupply with the immediate branch for a lower supply, linear interpolation in _totalSupplyComponents, pauseTransfer, setSelfDisableTransfer)
- https://sourcify.dev/server/v2/contract/999/0x104324863cfb2220756c60384efa9bb67a57aaf7?fields=sources (Verified wstHYPE implementation (exact match): balanceOf reads stHYPE.sharesOf, transfer calls stHYPE.transferShares)
- https://rpc.hyperliquid.xyz/evm eth_getLogs Transfer on both tokens in the 1,000 blocks ending near 51,956,900 (transaction hash starting 0x3d982e864a) and eth_call balanceOf of 0x40db8941e90c5a540c52159d9f3b9ad4417364b3 on both tokens (One transfer emitted 106.40 on stHYPE and 102.53 on wstHYPE between the same parties, and the receiver's two balances have the ratio 1.03777 of the two total supplies)
- https://rpc.hyperliquid.xyz/evm eth_call rebase() on 0xB96f07367e69e86d6e9C3F29215885104813eeAE from 0x00000000000000000000000000000000000a11ce and from the PoolManager; eth_getLogs Rebase at block 51,883,107 (rebase reverts with AccessControlUnauthorizedAccount for role 0x5fde63b5...0b75; the last rebase was sent by 0x4eb038eb501045daa520b972fcad48c429531e10 with selector 0xaf14052c and an interval of 82,800 seconds)
- https://rpc.hyperliquid.xyz/evm eth_call sharesOf(PoolManager), balanceOf(PoolManager) on both tokens, syncStart, syncEnd, rewardsToSync, totalSupply (The PoolManager holds no shares; interval 82,800 seconds, 123.56 HYPE of rewards on a supply of 2,275,453 stHYPE)

## F-068. xStocks tokens multiply every balance by a figure the issuer schedules, and the 8 for 1 split of SNDKx raised the PoolManager balance eightfold on Ethereum and X Layer with no transfer

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: Ethereum, BNB Smart Chain, Monad, X Layer.

What differs on the network: The Robinhood stock tokens cleared in an earlier cycle keep balanceOf fixed and change a display multiplier. The xStocks tokens (symbols ending in x, one implementation behind every proxy) do the opposite: balanceOf is shares times a multiplier, and the multiplier changes at a time the issuer sets. The PoolManager holds them on Ethereum (5 tokens in the window), BNB Smart Chain (42), Monad (4) and X Layer (7 over 6.9 days).

How it meets the code: 1. BackedAutoFeeTokenImplementation.balanceOf returns shares times getCurrentMultiplier() (lines 254 to 259). getCurrentMultiplier returns lastMultiplier until newMultiplierActivationTime and newMultiplier from that second on, reduced by feePerPeriod for each period that has passed (lines 265 to 283). Only the multiplier updater may schedule a change (lines 445 to 452) and only the owner may set feePerPeriod (lines 392 to 397). 2. SNDKx 0xb63efbc28860c8097e341de1fcf59456161e9d98 has a stored update from 1e18 to 8e18 with activation time 1795167000 (20 November 2026) on Ethereum and on X Layer. On X Layer the block with that timestamp is 76,397,964. The token emitted no log in that block, and balanceOf(PoolManager) went from 137,059,273,049,122,723 to 1,096,474,184,392,981,784 base units, a factor of exactly 8. 3. Pool accounting did not change (src/PoolManager.sol never reads a balance outside sync and settle), so seven eighths of the SNDKx the PoolManager now holds belong to no pool. A provider who had put in one token before the split can take out one token, which is worth one eighth. 4. A pool that still held liquidity at its old price after the activation time would quote eight times the market value for each token sold into it, and any account could sell new tokens there and take the other currency. I did not observe this: the pool that traded SNDKx before the split on X Layer had 1.04 SNDKx removed 21,405 seconds before activation, and later swaps went through another pool at about 26 units of the 6 decimal quote token per SNDKx, against about 267 before. 5. Dividends use the same path in small steps: STRCx has 26 stored updates and a multiplier of 1.1058, and each step raises the PoolManager balance without a transfer. feePerPeriod is 0 on the six token contracts I read on four networks; a value above 0 would lower every balance each period, which would leave pools short. 6. Transfers convert amounts to shares and back, which explains differences of 1 to 8 base units between Transfer logs and balances.

What is at risk: Every pool in an xStocks token on the four networks. Balances today: 569.57 STRCx, 17.34 NVDAx and 1.40 SNDKx on Ethereum; 1.88 SNDKx on X Layer; 42 tokens of up to 9.96 units each on BNB Smart Chain; a few thousand base units on Monad, where 14,932 NVDAx transfers passed through in a day. A split strands the added tokens in the PoolManager and cuts the value of the token side of each position by the split ratio. Each dividend strands the dividend. The other currency of the pool is exposed to whoever trades first after an activation time that is public in advance. I did not measure what share of the 569.57 STRCx is already unaccounted.

What it takes: The issuer schedules a multiplier change, which is a routine corporate action for these tokens, and liquidity stays in the pool past the activation time. An ordinary account cannot bracket the change with sync and settle or with take and repay, because the change happens at a timestamp or inside the updater's own transaction. The arbitrage in step 4 is my reading of the pool mechanics and was not seen on chain.

What can be done: Liquidity providers must remove liquidity before every scheduled activation and should prefer a wrapped form with a fixed balance. Interfaces should read newMultiplierActivationTime and multiplierUpdates and warn when a change is pending. A hook cannot recover the stranded tokens, because core has no payout for an unaccounted balance.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor value issuer is right: only the multiplier updater 0x5f7a4c11bde4f218f0025ef444c369d838ffa2ad or the owner can change the figure, and no ordinary account can place the change between a sync and a settle. Two details need correction. First, the sentence that one implementation sits behind every proxy does not hold on Monad: the SNDKx proxy there points to 0xd865ce1b07540b5ede20e8298f48da69770fe22e, 14,411 bytes, with no Sourcify match and no multiplierUpdates getter. I tested it from the PoolManager's own balance of 8 base units and 1 share: a transfer of 8 or 9 moves the one share, a transfer of 7 moves nothing and a transfer of 16 reverts, so its transfer uses the 8e18 multiplier as balanceOf does. SPYx, QQQx and NVDAx on Monad use the common implementation. Second, the downward case is wider than feePerPeriod. _updateMultiplier requires only a nonzero value (line 684), so the updater can lower the multiplier, for example for a reverse split, at once or at a set time, and the PoolManager would then hold less than pools record. The STRCx balance on Ethereum was 567.49 at my read. The arbitrage in step 4 remains unobserved, as the finding says.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:279-289`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-365`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`.

Sources:

- src/PoolManager.sol:279-289 (sync has no lock check and stores balanceOf(PoolManager) for any currency in transient storage.)
- src/PoolManager.sol:349-365 (_settle credits the recipient with the balance now minus the synced balance, with a checked subtraction, and resets the synced currency.)
- src/PoolManager.sol:291-297 (take debits the caller and transfers before anything is paid in.)
- https://sourcify.dev/server/v2/contract/1/0x65c40d624af3b18c109fbf87b7deff34cdc5f19b?fields=sources,abi,compilation (BackedAutoFeeTokenImplementation source, the EIP-1967 implementation of SNDKx on Ethereum: balanceOf, getCurrentMultiplier, updateMultiplierValue, updateFeePerPeriod and _transfer at the lines given.)
- https://rpc.xlayer.tech (eth_call balanceOf(PoolManager) on SNDKx at blocks 76,397,963 and 76,397,964: 137059273049122723 and 1096474184392981784. Block timestamps 1795166999 and 1795167000. eth_getLogs for the token in both blocks: none. multiplierUpdates(1) returns old 1e18, new 8e18, activation 1795167000. Swap logs before and after are in research/network-risk/cycles/004/theme-balance-reconcile.work/raw/xlayer_SNDKx_swaps.json.)

STRCx: multiplier 1105774914969160700, multiplierUpdatesLength 26, balance 569.568. feePerPeriod 0 and periodLength 604,800 on all tokens read. Values in research/network-risk/cycles/004/theme-balance-reconcile.work/raw/xstocks_multiplier.json.)

## F-035. Elysium USDC is a bridge representation whose backing on HyperEVM sits at one address that the issuer can block

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: Elysium.

What differs on the network: On Ethereum the PoolManager holds USDC issued by Circle directly. On Elysium the token called USDC is a standard gateway representation minted by the Arbitrum token bridge. Its backing is HyperEVM USDC held by one gateway contract on HyperEVM, and HyperEVM USDC has a blacklister role. The Elysium token itself has no block list.

How it meets the code: Step 1: the PoolManager on Elysium holds about 174,155 USDC at 0xb47c4C8F73F009CC0C0d3f92B388d13E293F0B65. The token reports the HyperEVM token 0xb88339CB7199b77E23DB6E890353E22632Ba630f as its parent and the ERC-20 gateway as its minter. Step 2: all 4.03M Elysium USDC are backed by 4.26M HyperEVM USDC held by the gateway 0xd97B30dE0bA0C2eAeE942afce77530e06c5F122c. Step 3: if the blacklister of HyperEVM USDC lists that gateway, every withdrawal from Elysium fails when the gateway tries to pay out, and deposits fail when it tries to pull tokens in. Step 4: on Elysium nothing reverts. sync, settle and take keep working for the representation because its transfer and balanceOf are unaffected, so pools keep quoting a token that can no longer be redeemed. Holders sell it into the pools and liquidity providers end up holding it. The PoolManager code treats the representation as any other ERC-20 and has no way to notice the change.

What is at risk: All pools on Elysium that use USDC, and ERC-6909 claims in it. The PoolManager balance was about 174,155 USDC. Liquidity providers on the other side of USDC pairs lose the assets that are sold to them for the impaired token. No funds are moved out of the PoolManager by this path.

What it takes: The blacklister of HyperEVM USDC, 0x33aa4e959132e49552cb754fb6585bdcdf874beb, must list the gateway. isBlacklisted(gateway) is false today. No unprivileged party can cause it. The same structure applies to every token bridged through the standard gateway whose parent chain contract has a block list.

What can be done: Liquidity providers should treat Elysium USDC as bridge exposure to one HyperEVM address and watch isBlacklisted for the gateway. Interfaces can label the token as a bridged representation. Nothing in the PoolManager can be configured against it.

Second reviewer's corrections: The figures moved since the research queries: the PoolManager balance is 182,936 USDC, and the gateway holds 4.27M against a supply of 4.05M. One further control exists that the finding does not list. The Elysium token is a beacon proxy (beacon 0x659f40efd07d70224b7ac61e4721f6f3e11b625e, implementation 0xbd080eef044a3e6226757487c5cef33568afefd6). The beacon owner is the proxy contract 0xce4ceced412aa9359840c7d52bbd99827a5d42b9, which can replace the logic of the token. I did not identify who controls that contract. This is a governance class control and does not change the actor or the severity.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:279-288`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-364`, `src/types/Currency.sol:53-75`.

Sources:

- https://rpc.elysium.kinetiq.xyz eth_call l1Address(), l2Gateway(), totalSupply(), isBlacklisted(address) on 0xb47c4C8F73F009CC0C0d3f92B388d13E293F0B65 and balanceOf(PoolManager), queried 2026-11-22 (The Elysium token is a gateway representation of the HyperEVM token, has a supply of 4.03M, has no isBlacklisted function, and the PoolManager holds about 174,155.)
- https://rpc.hyperliquid.xyz/evm eth_call balanceOf(gateway), blacklister(), isBlacklisted(gateway) on 0xb88339CB7199b77E23DB6E890353E22632Ba630f, queried 2026-11-22 (The gateway holds 4.26M HyperEVM USDC, the token has a blacklister, and the gateway is not listed today.)
- https://elysium.kinetiq.xyz/docs/bridging-hyperevm-tokens-to-elysium (The canonical bridge locks the token in the standard gateway on HyperEVM and mints a representation on Elysium, and lists the two USDC addresses.)

## F-073. The X Layer PoolManager is enrolled in USDG payout group 2, so every USDG transfer runs reward share accounting for it and the rewards go to a third address

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: X Layer.

What differs on the network: USDG is the Paxos reward token. Each account slot packs balance, shares, payout group id and last update time. On X Layer payoutGroupIdOf(PoolManager) returns 2. The claimer and the destination of group 2 are 0xb1b3d51e4be3fe55006d1ae4bc252dc6aa00fa72 and the group holds 23.18 million USDG in total. On Robinhood Chain, Ink, Arbitrum One and Ethereum the same call returns 0 for the PoolManager. A cleared item of cycle 3 states that enrolment needs the registrar role or the consent of the account. The PoolManager cannot give consent, so a role holder enrolled it on X Layer. I did not re-read the enrolment functions.

How it meets the code: 1. A take or a payment of USDG on X Layer runs _updateWalletWithPayoutGroup for the PoolManager: it recomputes the shares of the PoolManager with reward preservation, writes the timestamp and applies a share delta to the totals of group 2 (ClaimableRewardsBase.sol lines 572 to 619). On the other chains the transfer takes the short path that writes two balances. 2. I traced a full round trip on X Layer. The only slot that differed afterwards was the PoolManager slot, where the timestamp field moved from 0x6b03bb26 to 0x6b03bb81 and balance, shares and group id were unchanged. Twenty five round trips in one call all succeeded and left the balance unchanged. 3. Rounding in calcShares is downward on each step, so a round trip can cost the PoolManager slot up to a few share units of one millionth of a USDG. That reduces rewards of the group destination and does not touch the balance. 4. _applyDelta saturates at zero when it subtracts and the additions are bounded by 64 bit balances, so I found no revert that a caller can cause. 5. The rewards that accrue on the USDG that pools hold are paid to the destination of group 2 by claim. balanceOf(PoolManager) does not rise, so sync and settle cannot book them (src/PoolManager.sol:279-288 and 349-364). 6. The enrolment adds one revert path that the other chains do not have: _getMultiplierAtTime reverts with MultiplierIndexNotFound when the multiplier record of the group is empty. I did not establish whether an administrator can delete a multiplier that a live group still uses.

What is at risk: Liquidity providers of USDG pools on X Layer (385,837 USDG at the PoolManager at block 76,638,290) keep their principal. The reward on that balance accrues to 0xb1b3d51e4be3fe55006d1ae4bc252dc6aa00fa72. Transfers cost more gas than on the other chains. A fault in the multiplier configuration of group 2 would stop every USDG take and payment at the PoolManager, which adds to the issuer powers recorded in F-057.

What it takes: None for the reward routing, which is in place. The revert path needs an issuer side change of the multiplier of group 2, and that part is unconfirmed.

What can be done: Liquidity providers should know that USDG rewards on pooled balances on X Layer go to the group destination. The deployer can ask Paxos and the X Layer operator who controls group 2 and whether the PoolManager should stay enrolled.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor value issuer and the severity low stand. Additions. The enrolment happened on 2026-11-19 through the registrar batch function, which confirms the inference in the finding. The reward amount is close to nothing at the time of my query: getPayoutGroupAvailableRewards(2) returned 2,797 base units, 0.002797 USDG, on a group balance of 23.18 million, and availableRewardsOf(PoolManager) returned 0. The shares of the PoolManager are lower than its balance, so the multiplier has grown since enrolment, and the low figure fits a recent claim for the whole group. I did not find the claim event because the public endpoints limit eth_getLogs to 100 blocks. The statement that _applyDelta saturates is right for subtraction only. The addition is a checked uint64 addition and would revert above 18.4 trillion USDG, which the total supply of 1.0987 billion cannot reach. The comparison with Arbitrum One was not reproduced by me.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-364`, `src/types/Currency.sol:60-86`.

Sources:

- rpc https://rpc.xlayer.tech eth_call at block 76,638,290 on USDG 0x4ae46a509f6b1d9056937ba4500cb143933d2dc8 (payoutGroupIdOf(PoolManager) = 2, getPayoutGroupClaimer(2) and getPayoutGroupDestination(2) = 0xb1b3d51e4be3fe55006d1ae4bc252dc6aa00fa72, getPayoutGroupBalance(2) = 23.18 million USDG, getPayoutGroupMultId(2) = 2, availableRewardsOf(PoolManager) = 0, balanceOf(PoolManager) = 385,837.52 USDG. The same calls on Robinhood Chain, Ink, Arbitrum One and Ethereum return group 0.)
- rpc https://xlayer.drpc.org debug_traceCall at block 76,637,685 (State diff of a full round trip from the PoolManager address: one slot changed, 0x...6b03bb26000200000057ade4200b0000005953f44fee to 0x...6b03bb81000200000057ade4200b0000005953f44fee.)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/paxosglobal/paxos-token-contracts/master/contracts/ClaimableRewardsBase.sol (_transfer, _updateWalletWithPayoutGroup, _applyDelta (saturating subtraction), _getMultiplierAtTime (revert on an empty multiplier).)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/paxosglobal/paxos-token-contracts/master/contracts/lib/SharesLib.sol (calcShares, updateSharesWithRewardPreservation and calcSharesDelta round down.)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/paxosglobal/paxos-token-contracts/master/contracts/BaseStorageV3.sol (TokenAccountData packs balance (64 bits), shares (64 bits), payoutGroupId (16 bits) and lastUpdateTime (40 bits).)

## F-001. Operator transaction filtering on Robinhood Chain can fail any exit from the PoolManager, including force-included ones

High severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: sequencer, validator or bridge committee. Networks: Robinhood Chain.

What differs on the network: Ethereum has no party that can make a chosen transaction fail. Robinhood Chain runs the ArbOS 61 compliance filtering feature. Its sequencer drops transactions that involve an address on a restricted list, and an operator account can write any transaction hash into the ArbFilteredTransactionsManager precompile at 0x74. The state transition then skips that transaction, raises the sender's nonce and consumes its gas, even when the transaction was force-included from Ethereum. On chain, filtering has been enabled since 2026-06-24 10:00 UTC and one externally owned account, 0xebdc18a1f5c42fc25552ea233facf4054df224b7 with nonce 6,237, is the registered filterer.

How it meets the code: Every way to move funds out of the PoolManager is a call made inside unlock: modifyLiquidity with a negative delta, burn of ERC-6909 claims, and take. The contract has no exit that works without a successful child chain transaction. Step 1: the sequencer simulates each transaction and rejects it when it breaks a configured rule. The documented rule types cover a restricted sender or recipient, CALL, CREATE, CREATE2 or SELFDESTRUCT against a restricted address, and token transfers from or to one. Step 2: a user who then sends the transaction through the delayed inbox on Ethereum gets it included after the delay, but the filterer registers its hash first and RevertedTxHook skips the EVM call. The client code checks only whether the hash is in the list, so the list is whatever the filterer writes. Step 3: the user's liquidity and claims stay in the PoolManager with no other route out. The same rules mean that a take to a restricted recipient, or a hook or token that calls a restricted address on every operation, makes the transaction unacceptable to the sequencer. If the PoolManager, the PositionManager, or a widely used token or hook were put on the list, every transaction that calls it would be rejected. I could not confirm which rule set Robinhood has configured or whether any core contract is exempt, because the list is held off chain as salted hashes.

What is at risk: Funds are frozen for the affected sender or contract and cannot be taken by the filterer through this path. At the time of the query the PoolManager on this chain held 21,321 ETH, 16.98M USDG, 1,321 WETH and 3,134 ANT stock tokens. A listing of one LP freezes that LP's positions. A listing of a shared contract freezes every position that depends on it.

What it takes: The filterer account, the compliance list provider or the chain owner must act. No unprivileged party can add a hash or an address. A third party can cause rejection of someone else's transaction only if that transaction already executes code or transfers chosen by the third party, for example a hook that pays out to addresses the third party controls.

What can be done: Nothing in core can restore a forced exit. The deployer can ask the operator for a written commitment that the PoolManager and periphery addresses are never listed and can monitor FilteredTransactionAdded events on 0x74. Pool creators on this chain should avoid hooks that call or pay addresses supplied by third parties. LPs should treat force inclusion as unavailable when sizing positions.

Second reviewer's corrections: The severity stays high because the freeze needs the filterer account, the list provider or the chain owner. The profile quotes a dashboard figure of about 1,351 filtered hashes. The chain shows 6,234 add events from the one filterer account. The claims about a take to a restricted recipient and about hooks or tokens that touch a restricted address depend on the configured rule set, which is still unknown. The finding cites the precompile source at the v3.11.0 tag. I read that file at v3.11.3. A filtered deposit or retryable is redirected to 0xbbbd433db8700d8927faaaaf482e007e293ed568. That redirect moves bridged funds of the filtered sender and does not touch PoolManager balances.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:104-114`, `src/PoolManager.sol:145-184`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:332-336`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:131-137`.

Sources:

- https://docs.robinhood.com/chain/differences-from-ethereum/ (Robinhood Chain screens transactions at the sequencer and excludes any transaction associated with a sanctioned address.)
- https://docs.arbitrum.io/launch-arbitrum-chain/chain-config/sequencer/compliance-filtering (The rule set (origin, CALL and CREATE targets, token transfer events) and the precompile that fails delayed inbox transactions by hash.)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OffchainLabs/nitro/v3.11.3/arbos/tx_processor.go (RevertedTxHook skips execution, raises the nonce and consumes all gas when the transaction hash is in the on-chain filter. Deposits and retryables are redirected in StartTxHook.)
- https://github.com/OffchainLabs/nitro/blob/v3.11.0/precompiles/ArbFilteredTransactionsManager.go (A registered filterer can add or delete any transaction hash with no further check.)
- https://rpc.mainnet.chain.robinhood.com eth_call ArbOwnerPublic.getAllTransactionFilterers(), getTransactionFilteringFrom(), getFilteredFundsRecipient(); eth_getTransactionCount of the filterer; eth_getBalance and balanceOf of the PoolManager; queried 2026-11-22 (One filterer is registered, filtering started at timestamp 1782295200, the filterer has nonce 6,237, and the PoolManager balances are as stated.)
- https://ethbeat.org/layer2s/projects/robinhood (Independent statement that force inclusion on this chain can be nullified by transaction filtering.)

## F-046. X Layer voids blacklisted L1 deposits at the execution layer, so forced inclusion is no guaranteed exit from the PoolManager

High severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: sequencer, validator or bridge committee. Networks: X Layer.

What differs on the network: On Ethereum nobody can stop a valid transaction from executing once it is in a block. On a standard OP Stack chain a deposit sent through the OptimismPortal on Ethereum must be executed on L2 as written, which gives users an exit that the sequencer cannot refuse. The X Layer execution client adds a consensus rule: before it runs a user deposit it asks the TxBlacklist contract at 0x59055c0ef0be92018b33f877a3eB816355791727 whether the deposit's source hash is listed, and a listed deposit is recorded as a failed deposit without running its call. The contract is an upgradeable proxy. Its owner and the owner of its proxy admin are one account without code, 0x6EE7bDA7aF04F61ccf93aB4b8Db2289aBe76C6aa.

How it meets the code: 1. Every pool's funds leave the PoolManager through take inside unlock (src/PoolManager.sol:104-114, 291-297), and a liquidity position belongs to the msg.sender that created it (src/PoolManager.sol:159-167). An exit is therefore a transaction that must execute on X Layer from the position owner's address. 2. Cycle 1 recorded that the X Layer block builder can leave out sequencer submitted transactions through a risk control filter, and that an L1 deposit remained as the exit. 3. In the client source at tag xlayer/v0.1.7, OpEvm::transact_raw calls intercept_blacklisted_deposit before it executes a transaction. For a deposit that is not a system transaction and does not come from the L1 attributes depositor, it makes an uncommitted system call isBlacklisted(sourceHash) from 0xfffffffffffffffffffffffffffffffffffffffe to the address that the source fixes for chain id 196. 4. When the call returns true, the client builds the result through the handler's failed deposit path: every change of the transaction is reverted, the sender nonce is bumped, the mint is kept and the call to the target is never made. The deposit is included in the block and has no effect. 5. A source hash is known from the moment the deposit event is emitted on Ethereum. User deposits are executed in the first L2 block of the epoch whose origin is that Ethereum block, and the sequencer builds this block after the Ethereum block exists and may lag behind it inside the sequencing window. The party that controls the blacklist therefore has time to list the hash before the deposit is executed. 6. On chain, addToBlacklist(bytes32) reverts for an arbitrary sender, for the owner and for the PoolManager. The implementation has an operators(address) mapping and the setOperator(address,bool) selector, so the owner appoints the accounts that can list hashes. The same owner can replace the implementation through the proxy admin. 7. A liquidity provider whose ordinary transactions are filtered by the builder and whose deposits are listed has no remaining way to call the PoolManager. The code in src/ has no path that works without transaction execution.

What is at risk: Funds of any chosen liquidity provider, swapper or claim holder on X Layer can be held in the PoolManager for as long as the operator keeps filtering and listing. This covers 307.9 OKB and 5,889,016 USDT0 at the time of the query. Nothing is taken: the code has no time dependence, so the funds are intact when the block is lifted. The rule is part of the state transition that every X Layer node applies. The release notes say the same Rust stack is used for proof generation, so a proof would be expected to accept a voided deposit as correct. I did not read the proof program to confirm this.

What it takes: The holder of 0x6EE7bDA7aF04F61ccf93aB4b8Db2289aBe76C6aa, or an operator that this account appoints, must list each deposit's source hash before the deposit is executed, and the sequencer must also keep the same user's ordinary transactions out of blocks. An ordinary account cannot list a hash. Whether any hash has been listed could not be read, because the public endpoint limits log queries to 100 blocks. The tag xlayer/v0.1.7 is the op-node release that operators had to run by 10 November 2026. The exact submodule commit that the mainnet sequencer runs was not confirmed, and the blacklist file is also present at tag xlayer/v0.1.6.

What can be done: Liquidity providers should treat X Layer positions as dependent on one operator account and should not rely on L1 forced inclusion as an exit. The deployer can ask OKX to publish the conditions under which deposits are listed and to move ownership of the blacklist and its proxy admin to a multisignature account with a delay. Integrators can monitor the blacklist contract for operator changes and new entries.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor value operator is right: an ordinary account cannot list a hash and cannot make the sequencer filter a transaction. The balances moved since the research query. The PoolManager held 300.77 OKB and 5,945,282 USDT0 at block 76,601,162. Step 7 of the mechanism holds only while every transaction that could move the position is stopped. An account that already holds an ERC-6909 operator approval, an allowance or a PositionManager approval from the owner can act for the owner from a different sender, and the operator would have to filter or list that transaction as well. The finding says the rule is active by construction for chain id 196. That statement is true of the source at the release tag. Whether the running sequencer build pins that commit is unconfirmed, as the finding itself notes. Severity high follows the scale, since a freeze needs a privileged chain-level actor, and it matches the Robinhood Chain filter finding F-001.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:104-114`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:159-167`.

Sources:

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/okx/optimism/refs/tags/xlayer/v0.1.7/rust/alloy-op-evm/src/block/xlayer_blacklist_contract.rs (The mainnet blacklist address is fixed to 0x59055c0ef0be92018b33f877a3eB816355791727 for chain id 196. isBlacklisted(sourceHash) is called with transact_system_call from SYSTEM_ADDRESS. Only user deposits are eligible: system transactions and the L1 attributes depositor are excluded.)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/okx/optimism/refs/tags/xlayer/v0.1.7/rust/alloy-op-evm/src/lib.rs (transact_raw calls intercept_blacklisted_deposit before executing. On a hit it passes an invalid transaction error with the text 'deposit blacklisted' to the handler's catch_error and returns that result with the finalized state.)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/okx/optimism/refs/tags/xlayer/v0.1.7/rust/op-revm/src/handler.rs (catch_error for a deposit reverts to the first journal checkpoint, bumps the caller nonce, adds the mint, commits and returns the FailedDeposit halt.)
- https://rpc.xlayer.tech eth_getCode and eth_getStorageAt (EIP-1967 implementation and admin slots) of 0x59055c0ef0be92018b33f877a3eB816355791727; eth_call owner(), pendingOwner() on it and owner() on 0x57ffb399eb2d8fb1831001ba893483bc13bd8c2f; eth_getCode of 0x6EE7bDA7aF04F61ccf93aB4b8Db2289aBe76C6aa (The proxy has 1,059 bytes of code, implementation 0xe44bfef30cfd8ecbfb917d78fa2ac215b3397f8e and admin 0x57ffb399eb2d8fb1831001ba893483bc13bd8c2f. Both owner() calls return 0x6EE7bDA7aF04F61ccf93aB4b8Db2289aBe76C6aa, which has no code.)
- https://rpc.xlayer.tech eth_getCode of 0xe44bfef30cfd8ecbfb917d78fa2ac215b3397f8e; eth_call addToBlacklist(bytes32) from three senders, operators(address), isBlacklisted(bytes32) from 0xfffffffffffffffffffffffffffffffffffffffe (The implementation contains the selectors of isBlacklisted, blacklisted, addToBlacklist, removeFromBlacklist, operators and 0x558a7297. addToBlacklist reverts with 0x7c214f04 for every tested sender. isBlacklisted returns false for a test hash.)
- https://newreleases.io/project/github/okx/optimism/release/xlayer%2Fv0.1.7 (Operators had to run op-node xlayer/v0.1.7 together with xlayer-reth v0.0.7.3 by 10 November 2026.)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/okx/xlayer-reth/main/Cargo.toml (alloy-op-evm and op-revm are taken from the path deps/optimism/rust, a submodule of the OKX optimism repository.)
- https://specs.optimism.io/protocol/derivation.html (User deposited transactions are placed in the first L2 block of an epoch and are derived from the receipts of the L1 origin block. An L2 block timestamp is at or after the timestamp of its L1 origin, and the L2 blocks of an epoch are derived from the sequencing window that starts at that origin.)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/okx/xlayer-reth/main/crates/rcs-filter/src/lib.rs (The block builder screens each transaction against rules from an external risk control service and excludes transactions with a deny verdict.)

## F-020. HyperCore moves USDC out of the AQAv2 treasury address with unsigned transactions although that address has never signed anything, so a validator vote that named the PoolManager would need no act by it

Medium severity, plausible. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: sequencer, validator or bridge committee. Networks: HyperEVM.

What differs on the network: HyperCore injects system transactions into HyperEVM blocks. They carry no signature, pay no gas and the node sets the sender. Under AQAv2 the node sends such transactions in the name of two addresses, the linked contract of the quote token and a treasury address that the treasury deployer designates, to hold their token balances in a 1 to 9 ratio. Activation is by validator vote. On Ethereum a balance leaves an address only through a transaction that the address signed or a call that its own code made.

How it meets the code: 1. Earlier cycles could not tell whether the designated treasury address has to agree on chain. The chain answers this. The USDC treasury address 0xc20699185c15D0a2fD65779BB5d69f5b0B113c00 had nonce 0 and 0 HYPE at block 37,597,802, so it had never sent an EVM transaction and could not have paid for one. Its first transaction, in block 37,597,803 on 2026-06-12, is a system transaction with nonce 0, gas price 0 and no signature that calls transfer on USDC and moves 2,001 USDC to the CoreDepositWallet. The HyperCore info API reports that the address does not exist as a HyperCore user and has never sent a HyperCore action. The authorizeAqav2Role action has the fields token and role only, and the staking account that sent it for Coinbase is a different address. No on-chain act by the treasury address preceded the transfers. Coinbase named both addresses in public, which is consent outside the protocol. 2. The node also sends USDC transfers in the name of the CoreDepositWallet, which is a contract. A system transaction can therefore carry a contract address as sender. 3. If a validator vote activated AQAv2 for a token with the PoolManager as treasury address, the node would send transfer calls on that token with the PoolManager as msg.sender whenever the 9 to 1 rule calls for a transfer toward the linked contract. The token treats them as transfers by the holder. 4. Nothing in src sees this. Pool accounting is never compared with a token balance. sync reads the balance only to open a measuring window (src/PoolManager.sol:279-288) and settle credits only a rise inside that window (src/PoolManager.sol:349-365). The currency's pools and claims would still record the tokens as owed, and the last takes of that currency would revert in the token transfer (src/types/Currency.sol:55-87). 5. What I could not find out: whether HyperCore checks the designated address in any way, for example refuses an address with code, whether the treasury deployer can change the address after activation, and the exact rebalancing rule. No public source states them and the validator node is closed source.

What is at risk: All pools and ERC-6909 claims in a quote token whose AQAv2 treasury address were set to the PoolManager. USDC is the only AQAv2 token today, its treasury address is Coinbase's, and the PoolManager holds 997.75 USDC. An ordinary account cannot bring this about.

What it takes: A treasury deployer and a technical deployer that each stake 500,000 HYPE, and a validator vote that activates AQAv2 with the PoolManager as treasury address or changes an existing designation to it. The balance of the token's linked contract must sit below its 10 percent share for transfers to flow out of the treasury address.

What can be done: Ask the Hyperliquid team whether a treasury designation is checked against the named address and whether it can change after activation. Watch eth_getSystemTxsByBlockNumber for a system transaction whose sender is the PoolManager. Liquidity providers in AQAv2 quote tokens depend on the validator set for this in the same way as for any other system transaction.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor operator is right. The step needs a validator vote and two deployers with 500,000 staked HYPE each, and an ordinary account can only change the size and timing of transfers between the two addresses that are already fixed for USDC. The title states that a vote naming the PoolManager would need no act by it. The evidence supports a narrower statement: the named address sent no EVM transaction and no HyperCore action before transfers left it. A proof of control carried inside the deployer's action, or a rule against addresses with code, is not excluded, so the verdict stays plausible and medium. One more fact belongs with this finding. The official replay client derives the sender of a system transaction as 0x2222222222222222222222222222222222222222 for empty input and as the system address mapped to the target contract otherwise (hyper-evm-sync src/run.rs lines 223 to 227 on the main branch). It has no way to produce the treasury address or the CoreDepositWallet as sender. The archive client reads the sender from the synthetic signature field s and accepts any address there. The public evidence on how the validator node treats these senders is therefore the archive client and chain data only.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:279-288`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-365`, `src/types/Currency.sol:55-87`.

Sources:

- https://rpc.purroofgroup.com eth_getTransactionCount, eth_getBalance and eth_call USDC balanceOf for 0xc20699185c15D0a2fD65779BB5d69f5b0B113c00 at blocks 37,597,802 and 37,597,803; eth_getBlockByNumber 37,597,803 (Nonce 0, 0 HYPE and 4,397,021,956.898190 USDC before the block; the first transaction has nonce 0, gas price 0 and gas 200,000 and calls transfer(0x6b9e773128f453f5c2c60935ee2de2cbc5390a24, 2001000000) on USDC; the balance fell by 2,001 USDC)
- https://rpc.hyperliquid.xyz/evm eth_getSystemTxsByBlockNumber 0x23db26b (The official node lists the same transaction as a system transaction with r 0 and s 0 and the treasury address as sender)
- https://api.hyperliquid.xyz/info userRole, preTransferCheck, delegatorSummary for 0xc20699185c15D0a2fD65779BB5d69f5b0B113c00 and 0x4E5319dEb1072B01439EE674db5C321d11fd96F8 (Treasury address: role missing, userExists false, userHasSentTx false, no stake. Staking account: role user, 506,986.51 HYPE delegated)
- https://hyperliquid.gitbook.io/hyperliquid-docs/hypercore/aligned-quote-assets.md (The treasury deployer designates a treasury address; activation is by validator vote after both deployers staked and sent the authorization from the staking accounts; the HyperEVM balance is held 9 to 1 between the treasury address and the linked contract)
- https://hyperliquid.gitbook.io/hyperliquid-docs/for-developers/api/exchange-endpoint.md (authorizeAqav2Role takes token and role and no address)
- https://hyperliquid.xyz/article/aqav2 (Coinbase is the treasury deployer and Circle the technical deployer for USDC)
- https://cryptoadventure.com/coinbase-activates-hyperliquid-usdc-treasury-wallet-as-aqav2-rollout-advances/ (Coinbase named 0x4E5319dEb1072B01439EE674db5C321d11fd96F8 and 0xc20699185c15D0a2fD65779BB5d69f5b0B113c00 as its two AQAv2 addresses. This is a news site and I did not find Coinbase's own post)
- https://rpc.purroofgroup.com eth_getBlockByNumber for 3,000 blocks ending near block 51,995,930 (224 system transactions sent in the name of the treasury address and 477 in the name of the CoreDepositWallet contract, each a USDC transfer with 200,000 gas and gas price 0)

## F-009. Thirteen Wormhole guardians can act as PoolManager owner on BNB Smart Chain and Avalanche

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: sequencer, validator or bridge committee. Networks: BNB Smart Chain, Avalanche.

What differs on the network: On Ethereum the owner is the governance timelock itself. On BNB Smart Chain and Avalanche the owner is a Uniswap Wormhole message receiver contract. It executes calls carried in a Wormhole message whose emitter is the Uniswap sender contract on Ethereum. A Wormhole message is valid with 13 of 19 guardian signatures, so the guardian set is a second party that can produce owner calls.

How it meets the code: 1. owner() of the PoolManager is 0x341c1511141022cf8ee20824ae0ffa3491f1302b on BNB Smart Chain and 0x47eb0cf11a1626462da3c830bcde64c3f582b5a6 on Avalanche. Both contracts carry the strings 'Uniswap Wormhole Message Receiver', 'Invalid Emitter Address!' and 'Sub-call failed', and messageSender() returns 0xf5f4496219f31cdcba6130b5402873624585615a. 2. A message that passes the receiver's checks makes it call arbitrary targets. As owner it can call setProtocolFeeController (src/ProtocolFees.sol:29-32). 3. The controller can set a protocol fee on any pool up to 1000 pips per direction (src/ProtocolFees.sol:35-41, src/libraries/ProtocolFeeLibrary.sol:25-32) and can withdraw accrued protocol fees to any recipient (src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-57). The withdrawal is bounded by protocolFeesAccrued through a checked subtraction at src/ProtocolFees.sol:55. 4. On BNB Smart Chain the receiver is also owner and feeSetter of the current fee adapter. On Avalanche no controller is set, so the first message through this path would name one. 5. No owner or controller function in src/ reaches pool reserves or ERC-6909 claims. The Owned base contract comes from lib/solmate, which is an empty directory in this checkout, so its transfer function was not read.

What is at risk: Accrued protocol fees and future protocol fees of at most 0.1% of swap input per direction on every pool of the chain. Swappers pay the fee. Liquidity provider principal is not reachable through this path.

What it takes: Thirteen of nineteen Wormhole guardians sign a message that Ethereum governance did not send, or the Wormhole core contract on the chain is compromised. How the receiver verifies signatures, sequence numbers and the 172800 second timeout was inferred from bytecode strings and getters. The receiver source was not read.

What can be done: Monitor ProtocolFeeControllerUpdated and ProtocolFeeUpdated events on both chains and compare them with executed Ethereum governance proposals. Keep accrued protocol fees low by collecting often. Governance can move ownership to a receiver with a second independent attestation if the fee stream grows.

Second reviewer's corrections: The finding says the receiver checks were inferred from bytecode strings. For BNB Smart Chain they are now confirmed from verified source, including the 2 day timeout and the strictly increasing sequence. The 13 of 19 threshold is confirmed on chain through quorum(19) on the core contract the receiver calls. On Avalanche no controller exists, so the first accepted message would also name the first controller, and no protocol fee can accrue before that. Low stands. The reachable funds are accrued protocol fees and a fee of at most 0.1% per direction on later swaps. Liquidity provider principal and ERC-6909 claims are out of reach.

Code: `src/ProtocolFees.sol:29-32`, `src/ProtocolFees.sol:35-41`, `src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-57`, `src/libraries/ProtocolFeeLibrary.sol:25-32`.

Sources:

- rpc https://bsc-dataseed1.bnbchain.org, queries near block 135065254 on 2026-11-22 (PoolManager slot 0 and slot 2; owner bytecode strings; messageSender(), NAME() and MESSAGE_TIME_OUT_SECONDS() on the owner; owner() and feeSetter() of the fee adapter)
- rpc https://api.avax.network/ext/bc/C/rpc, queries near block 101062173 on 2026-11-22 (PoolManager slot 0 is the receiver and slot 2 is zero; messageSender(), chainId() = 6 and nextMinimumSequence() = 0 on the owner)
- https://wormhole.com/docs/protocol/infrastructure/guardians/ (19 guardians, and a valid message needs 13 signatures)

## F-010. Polygon state sync path lets the Heimdall validator set act as PoolManager owner

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: sequencer, validator or bridge committee. Networks: Polygon.

What differs on the network: On Polygon the PoolManager owner is a proxy that trusts FxChild to report the Ethereum sender of a message. FxChild trusts the StateReceiver at 0x1001, and Bor calls the StateReceiver from a system address with records that Heimdall validators attest. Ethereum has no system caller that can stand in for the timelock.

How it meets the code: 1. owner() of the PoolManager is 0x8a1b966ac46f42275860f905dbc75efbfdc12374. Its fxChild() is 0x8397259c983751daf40400790063935a11afa28a and its l1Owner() is the Ethereum timelock 0x1a9c8182c09f50c8318d769245bea52c32be35bc. 2. FxChild.onStateReceive requires msg.sender to be 0x1001, decodes the root sender and receiver from the record data and calls processMessageFromRoot on the receiver. 3. Bor delivers records in Finalize through a system call from 0xffffFFFfFFffffffffffffffFfFFFfffFFFfFFfE to 0x1001. The records come from Heimdall. 4. A record whose data names the timelock as root sender makes the proxy execute arbitrary calls as PoolManager owner. It can call setProtocolFeeController (src/ProtocolFees.sol:29-32). The controller then sets fees up to 1000 pips per direction (src/ProtocolFees.sol:35-41, src/libraries/ProtocolFeeLibrary.sol:25-32) and withdraws accrued fees (src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-57). The same proxy is owner and feeSetter of the current fee adapter. 5. The code at 0x1001 is itself replaced by hard forks. Uburu did so on 2026-11-05 through a release that validators ran before the source was public.

What is at risk: Accrued protocol fees (536 POL, 134 USDC and 0.03 WETH at the time of the query, among others) and future protocol fees of at most 0.1% of swap input per direction. Liquidity provider principal is not reachable through owner or controller functions in src/.

What it takes: A Heimdall majority attests a state sync record that Ethereum did not emit, or a client release changes the StateReceiver or the system call path. The share of Heimdall stake needed was not confirmed from a source and is assumed to be two thirds. The proxy source and the post-Uburu StateReceiver code were not read. The proxy checks were inferred from its three selectors and its revert strings.

What can be done: Monitor ProtocolFeeControllerUpdated and ProtocolFeeUpdated on Polygon against executed Ethereum proposals. Review each Bor release that touches 0x1001 or state sync execution. Collect protocol fees often so little sits in the PoolManager.

Second reviewer's corrections: 1. The precondition is too narrow. Five of the nine signers of the Safe that owns the StateSender on Ethereum can register a new sender for FxChild and emit a StateSynced record whose data names the timelock as root sender. Honest Heimdall validators would relay it. No Heimdall majority and no client release is needed. 2. The two thirds Heimdall threshold, which the finding assumed, is confirmed by Polygon documentation. 3. The proxy checks, which the finding inferred from strings, are confirmed from verified source. 4. The post-Uburu StateReceiver source is public in the genesis-contracts repository, and its function set matches the 20 selectors deployed at 0x1001. It has three replay functions for failed records that any address can call. This is recorded as a lead. 5. Severity stays low because only the protocol fee stream is reachable and liquidity provider principal is out of reach. If the review counts future protocol fees as pool funds, the 5 of 9 path would justify medium.

Code: `src/ProtocolFees.sol:29-32`, `src/ProtocolFees.sol:35-41`, `src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-57`, `src/libraries/ProtocolFeeLibrary.sol:25-32`.

Sources:

- rpc https://polygon.drpc.org, queries at block 97759148 to 97759199 on 2026-11-22 (PoolManager slot 0 and slot 2; fxChild() and l1Owner() of the owner; owner bytecode strings; owner() and feeSetter() of the fee adapter; protocolFeesAccrued values read with eth_getStorageAt)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/0xPolygon/fx-portal/main/contracts/FxChild.sol (onStateReceive requires msg.sender 0x1001 and forwards the decoded root sender to processMessageFromRoot)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/0xPolygon/bor/v2.11.0/consensus/bor/statefull/processor.go (System messages are sent from BorSystemAddress with zero gas price by a direct EVM call)
- https://github.com/0xPolygon/bor/releases/tag/v2.11.0 (State sync records are fetched from Heimdall; Uburu replaced the StateReceiver genesis contract at the fork block and shipped first as a private release)

## F-016. Thirteen Wormhole guardians can act as PoolManager owner on Monad and MegaETH

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: sequencer, validator or bridge committee. Networks: Monad, MegaETH.

What differs on the network: On Ethereum the PoolManager owner is the governance timelock itself. On Monad and MegaETH the owner is a Uniswap Wormhole Message Receiver contract (0xe783de89a7f0408687f051e3e6d0beb62719ebad and 0xa107580f73bd797bd8b87ff24e98346d99f93ddb). It executes any list of calls carried by a Wormhole message whose emitter is the Uniswap Wormhole Message Sender on Ethereum (0xf5f4496219f31cdcba6130b5402873624585615a, owned by the timelock). Whether such a message is genuine is decided by the Wormhole core contract on each chain, which accepts a signature set from 13 of 19 guardians and is itself an upgradeable proxy governed by guardian-signed messages. The established-l1 unit reported the same path for BNB Smart Chain and Avalanche in this cycle.

How it meets the code: 1. setProtocolFeeController is restricted to the owner (src/ProtocolFees.sol:29-32). The owner modifier comes from solmate's Owned, imported at src/ProtocolFees.sol:9. lib/solmate is empty in this checkout, so that file could not be read. 2. Thirteen guardians can sign a message that names the Ethereum sender as emitter, chain 2 as origin and the receiver on Monad (Wormhole chain 48) or MegaETH (chain 64) as destination. The receiver bytecode contains checks for emitter address, emitter chain, sequence number, a 172800 second lifetime, destination contract and destination chain. All of them are fields of the signed message. 3. The receiver then calls the PoolManager as owner and sets a controller chosen by the signers. 4. The controller sets a protocol fee of up to 1000 pips per direction on any pool (src/ProtocolFees.sol:35-41, src/libraries/ProtocolFeeLibrary.sol:8 and 25-32) and withdraws accrued fees to any address (src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-57). 5. The withdrawal is bounded by protocolFeesAccrued through a checked subtraction at src/ProtocolFees.sol:55, so pool reserves and claims are out of reach. 6. The Wormhole core at both addresses has a non-empty EIP-1967 implementation slot, so a guardian governance message can also replace the verification logic.

What is at risk: Protocol fees only. The controller is the zero address on both networks and protocolFeesAccrued is zero for the currencies checked, so nothing can be withdrawn today. After a takeover the signers could divert up to 0.1 percent of swap input per direction on every pool from then on. Liquidity provider principal and ERC-6909 claims are unaffected, and no operation is blocked.

What it takes: Thirteen of the nineteen guardians in guardian set 7 must sign a forged message, or the guardian governance path must upgrade the core contract. An unprivileged attacker cannot do either. The honest path needs a Uniswap governance vote through the Ethereum timelock.

What can be done: Treat the guardian set as part of PoolManager governance on these networks. Monitor ProtocolFeeControllerUpdated events and owner() on both PoolManagers and upgrades of the two Wormhole core proxies. A governance message through the same path can reset the controller after an incident, provided ownership was not transferred.

Second reviewer's corrections: 1. The 13 of 19 threshold is confirmed on chain through quorum(19) on the core contract each receiver embeds. 2. nextMinimumSequence is 0 on both receivers, so no governance message has been executed there yet and the first accepted message would name the first controller. 3. The timeout check reads the block timestamp, which is volatile data on MegaETH. This only lets a caller make its own receiveMessage call revert. 4. Low stands. Nothing has accrued and no controller exists. After a takeover the reach is a fee of at most 0.1 percent of swap input per direction on later swaps, paid by swappers. The established-l1 verdict for the same receiver design on BNB Smart Chain and Avalanche reached the same rating.

Code: `src/ProtocolFees.sol:29-32`, `src/ProtocolFees.sol:35-41`, `src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-57`, `src/libraries/ProtocolFeeLibrary.sol:8`, `src/libraries/ProtocolFeeLibrary.sol:25-32`.

Sources:

- rpc https://rpc.monad.xyz, queries at blocks 124417992 to 124420045 on 2026-11-22 (owner() and protocolFeeController() of the PoolManager; receiver name, embedded Wormhole core 0x194b123c5e96b9b2e49763619985790dc241cac0, sender, chain IDs and lifetime; core chainId 48, guardian set index 7 with 19 guardian addresses, implementation slot; protocolFeesAccrued)
- rpc https://mainnet.megaeth.com/rpc, queries at blocks 32548426 to 32549054 on 2026-11-22 (owner() and protocolFeeController() of the PoolManager; receiver name, embedded Wormhole core 0xabf89de706b583424328b54dd05a8fc986750da8, sender, chain IDs and lifetime; core chainId 64, guardian set index 7 with 19 guardian addresses, implementation slot; protocolFeesAccrued)
- rpc https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com, eth_call and eth_getStorageAt at latest on 2026-11-22 (The sender 0xf5f4496219f31cdcba6130b5402873624585615a is named Uniswap Wormhole Message Sender and is owned by the timelock 0x1a9c8182c09f50c8318d769245bea52c32be35bc)
- https://wormhole.com/docs/protocol/infrastructure/guardians/ (19 guardians, 13 signatures required for a valid message)
- https://wormhole.com/docs/reference/contract-addresses/ (Wormhole core addresses on Monad and MegaETH match the addresses embedded in the receivers)

## F-017. MegaETH has one sequencer, so an exit from the PoolManager that it refuses to include depends on L1 forced inclusion with a 12 hour bound

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: sequencer, validator or bridge committee. Networks: MegaETH.

What differs on the network: Ethereum has many block proposers and no party that can keep a valid transaction out for long. MegaETH has a single sequencer that decides ordering and inclusion and sets the transaction gas cap and size limits by configuration. The only path around it is a deposit transaction through the OptimismPortal on Ethereum, which the documentation says must be included within 12 hours. The L1 contracts that define this path are upgradeable by one Safe with a threshold of 6 of 10 owners, and L2BEAT reports that upgrades have no delay and that only one whitelisted proposer can publish state roots.

How it meets the code: 1. Every withdrawal of pool funds is a transaction that reaches unlock (src/PoolManager.sol:104-114) and then take (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297) or burn. The PoolManager has no path that works without a transaction on MegaETH. 2. The sequencer can leave such a transaction out. A liquidity provider then has to send it through depositTransaction on L1. It executes with the L1 sender as msg.sender, aliased if that sender is a contract, so positions owned by an externally owned account or by a contract the user can call from that account remain reachable. 3. During the delay of up to 12 hours the sequencer can include other transactions first. It can trade against the position that is trying to leave, and it can run any hook or token call that the exit depends on. 4. Forced transactions still run under MegaEVM limits. User deposits are not exempt from the compute gas, data, KV update and state growth ceilings, and the sequencer-configured gas cap can change without a fork. Whether a forced transaction can be delayed further by the per-block state growth cap was not determined. 5. A change of the portal or of the derivation rules by the 6 of 10 Safe, or a fork that patches system contract code, could remove the forced path. That requires the chain operator to act against its users and is outside what the PoolManager code can influence.

What is at risk: Liquidity operations and swaps can be delayed by up to 12 hours for selected users or for all users. No funds move without a valid transaction, so the direct effect is a delay and adverse execution during it. At the time of the queries the MegaETH PoolManager held 2.26 ETH, 14.1 thousand USDm and 30.8 thousand MEGA. A hostile upgrade of the L1 contracts would be a wider failure of the chain and is not specific to the PoolManager.

What it takes: The sequencer operator must censor or stall. For anything beyond a 12 hour delay the 6 of 10 Safe or the node software must change the forced inclusion rules. No unprivileged actor can do this. One lead remains open: the per-block state growth cap of 1,000 entries might let a paying spammer shorten blocks for everyone.

What can be done: Integrators should document the L1 forced inclusion route for PositionManager calls and test it on the Sepolia deployment. Liquidity providers who hold positions through contracts should check that the contract can be driven from an L1 deposit. Monitor upgrades of the OptimismPortal proxy and changes of the SequencerRegistry roles.

Second reviewer's corrections: 1. Severity is lowered from medium to low. The confirmed effect is a delay of at most 12 hours for transactions the sequencer leaves out, with a documented route around it and no loss path. Trading against a delayed position is ordering advantage, which the review rules exclude unless it ends in loss or freeze. Comparable sequencer findings in this cycle (Soneium, X Layer) were rated low. A lasting freeze needs the 6 of 10 Safe or a fork to remove the forced path, which is a failure of the whole chain. 2. The finding leaves open which limit bounds a forced transaction. The portal's resource configuration read on L1 caps deposit gas at 20,000,000 per L1 block, and that budget is shared by all deposits in the block. The SystemConfig gasLimit of 60,000,000 is a separate value. A normal removal fits. The interaction with MegaETH storage gas is recorded as a lead. 3. The PoolManager balances quoted in the impact were re-read only for native ETH (2.24 ETH) and USDm (14,055).

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:104-114`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:332-336`.

Sources:

- https://docs.megaeth.com/architecture.md (The sequencer is the single node that decides transaction ordering; data goes to EigenDA; disputes use Kailua)
- https://docs.megaeth.com/user-guide/force-inclusion.md (Deposits through the OptimismPortal 0x7f82f57F0Dd546519324392e408b01fcC7D709e8 must be included within 12 hours)
- https://specs.optimism.io/protocol/deposits.html (Deposit execution, sender aliasing for contracts and the unconditional mint)
- https://docs.megaeth.com/developer-docs/overview-3/resource-limits.md (The gas cap and transaction size are sequencer-configured and can change without a hardfork; per-block caps seal the block)
- https://docs.megaeth.com/spec/system-contracts/system-tx.md (User deposit transactions are never system-originated and get no metering exemption)
- rpc https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com, eth_call and eth_getStorageAt at latest on 2026-11-22 (Portal ProxyAdmin owner is a Safe with threshold 6 and ten owners; respectedGameType 1337; guardian 0xb2a9eb0c7b729c3ec704e843ef260084b3cae67f; paused() false)
- https://l2beat.com/scaling/projects/megaeth (No upgrade delay, single whitelisted proposer, 12 hour self-sequencing delay, not a Stage 0 project (secondary source))

## F-022. The owner on HyperEVM is a Wormhole message receiver, so a guardian quorum can exercise the owner role

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: sequencer, validator or bridge committee. Networks: HyperEVM.

What differs on the network: On Ethereum the owner acts through the governance timelock directly. On HyperEVM the owner is the contract 0x2d09d0c2f82c59b19b3c65a48ce2c550bf0921f9, which names itself Uniswap Wormhole Message Receiver. It executes a list of target calls taken from a Wormhole message whose emitter is 0xf5f4496219f31cdcba6130b5402873624585615a on Wormhole chain 2. The message is valid when 13 of the 19 Wormhole guardians have signed it, and anyone can submit it. The protocol fee controller is currently the zero address.

How it meets the code: 1. setProtocolFeeController is restricted to the owner (src/ProtocolFees.sol:29-32). 2. The controller can set a protocol fee on any pool up to 1000 pips in each direction (src/ProtocolFees.sol:35-41, src/libraries/ProtocolFeeLibrary.sol:8 and 25-32) and can withdraw accrued protocol fees to any recipient (src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-57). 3. The withdrawal is capped by protocolFeesAccrued through a checked subtraction (src/ProtocolFees.sol:54-55), and that counter grows only by the protocol share of swap fees (src/PoolManager.sol:238, src/libraries/Pool.sol:385-398). 4. A party that can produce a valid Wormhole message from the expected emitter, or 13 colluding or compromised guardians, can make the receiver call setProtocolFeeController with an address of its choice, then set the maximum fee on every pool and collect it. 5. The receiver runs arbitrary target calls, so the same party can also make the owner address call other contracts, including CoreWriter. Owned.sol is not present in this checkout, so the ownership transfer function could not be read. The deployed bytecode contains the transferOwnership(address) selector.

What is at risk: Up to 0.1 percent of swap input in each direction on every pool is diverted from LPs and swappers for as long as the hostile controller stays in place. LP principal, ERC-6909 claims and reserves are out of reach of the owner and the controller in the code read.

What it takes: A quorum of 13 of 19 Wormhole guardians signs a forged message, or the Ethereum side sender or Uniswap governance is compromised. The Wormhole core contract on HyperEVM at 0x7c0fafc4384551f063e05aee704ab943b8b53ab3 reports guardian set index 7 with 19 guardians.

What can be done: The deployer can note the bridge dependency in deployment documentation and monitor ProtocolFeeControllerUpdated and ProtocolFeeUpdated events on HyperEVM. LPs can watch slot0 protocol fee values of their pools. The loss is bounded by the fee cap in the code.

Second reviewer's corrections: The finding says the ownership transfer function could not be read. The pinned solmate file shows that the owner can also hand ownership to any address, so a forged message can make the takeover permanent until governance notices, though the reach stays limited to protocol fees. The effect is a diversion of fees and matches what other units label siphon.
